English for Work · Customers & creative work

Hairdressing and Barbering English

Clarify haircut preferences, discuss color expectations, manage salon appointments, check comfort, explain charges, and resolve service misunderstandings.

8 practical lessonsIntermediate to advanced1 complete learner book

Words into action

Make yourself clear.

For Hairstylists, barbers, salon assistants, and salon reception staff.

Copy complete AI prompts ↓

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Separate a reference image into chosen and unchosen features, then confirm the agreed length before cutting.
  • Compare visible features, clarify an everyday styling limit, and distinguish a style label from an agreed result.
  • Clarify a color goal and previous-product uncertainty while explaining what a consultation has not yet established.
  • Explain a delay, calculate the earliest ordinary finish, and offer a reschedule without promising a shortened service.
  • Acknowledge discomfort, clarify the client description, and arrange a check without diagnosing or prescribing a position.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Your eight lessons

01 Turning a reference photo into a clear request02 Describing shape, texture, and everyday styling03 Discussing color without promising a photograph04 Booking time and handling appointment changes05 Checking comfort and pausing clearly06 Explaining services, prices, and product choices07 Responding to a concern about the result08 Passing preferences to the next appointment
Prompts

All salons, clients, prices, photographs described in words, and incidents are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not cutting, chemical services, medical assessment, product testing, or legal requirements. Follow actual professional training, local rules, product instructions, consultation and consent procedures, and emergency arrangements. No example authorizes an unassessed chemical service, continuing through discomfort, or using a client image without the relevant permission.

01Turning a reference photo into a clear requestKeep the ponytail length+

Your goal

Separate a reference image into chosen and unchosen features, then confirm the agreed length before cutting.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

Keep the ponytail length

Client Alex shows stylist Ren a photograph of a chin-length bob with a straight fringe. Alex likes the outline but wants enough length for a low ponytail. No cut has started. Through clarification, they agree to discuss a collarbone-length outline rather than copying the chin-length endpoint. Alex confirms that no fringe is requested. Ren must keep the visual reference, practical length requirement, and agreed plan distinct. The conversation does not guarantee that every strand will tie back or authorize other changes not specifically agreed.

Alex: Client / Ren: Stylist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What practical feature matters to Alex?

Answer and explanation

D. Enough retained length for a low ponytail

Alex wants to retain practical length rather than copy the photograph exactly.

2. Which endpoint is agreed for the outline discussion?

Answer and explanation

B. Collarbone length

The clarification establishes collarbone length instead of the reference's chin-length endpoint.

3. What is the fringe status?

Answer and explanation

B. No fringe is requested

Alex explicitly confirms that the fringe in the reference is not requested.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The photo has a chin-length endpoint and a straight fringe. Alex chooses neither automatically. The agreed outline discussion is collarbone-length, with no fringe requested and retained ponytail length as the practical requirement.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
consultation
Discussion to establish preferences, suitability, and the proposed service.conduct a consultation
reference photo
Image used to communicate an idea, not automatically a complete instruction.discuss the reference photo
bob
Short-to-medium haircut family with a defined outline, varying in length and shape.clarify the bob outline
outline
Outer visible shape or boundary of the haircut.confirm the outline
perimeter
Outer edge defining the haircut's overall length and shape.discuss the perimeter
chin length
Length described relative to the chin.distinguish chin length
collarbone length
Length described relative to the collarbone.confirm collarbone length
retained length
Hair length kept rather than removed.preserve retained length
low ponytail
Hair gathered and secured low at the back of the head.retain low-ponytail length
fringe
Front hair shaped to fall over or around the forehead; also called bangs.clarify the fringe
bangs
Common US term for a fringe.explain bangs
straight fringe
Fringe with a visually straight outline across the front.identify the straight fringe
face-framing
Hair shaped around the face as a distinct design feature.discuss face-framing
front length
Length retained in the hair toward the face.protect front length
side length
Length of hair at the sides of the head.compare side length
back length
Length of hair at the back of the head.confirm back length
parting
Line or division where hair is separated; also called a part.identify the parting
blunt outline
Perimeter with a visually solid, even edge.describe a blunt outline
layers
Hair lengths arranged at different levels within a haircut.discuss layers
trim
Removal of a limited amount, which still needs an agreed length.clarify the trim
length removal
Amount of hair to be cut off.confirm length removal
shrinkage
Shorter visible length when curls recoil from a stretched or wet state.allow for visible shrinkage
agreed plan
Service details explicitly confirmed with the client.repeat the agreed plan
consent
Permission for the specified action, not every possible variation.confirm service consent

A trim, a bob, and enough to tie back can mean different things to different people. Use an agreed visible length reference and check the actual hair before making a promise about how every section will behave.

C Notice the language

A picture starts the conversation

A client may expect a photograph to explain a preference but may not want every feature copied. Ask which part appeals to them, then point out any conflict with a practical requirement. Confirm the plan in ordinary length words before relying on technical style names.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Open the reference
Which features of this photograph do you like?
Separate length and shape
Do you want the outline, the length, or both?
Identify the visible endpoint
The photograph shows a chin-length bob.
Ask about daily use
Do you need to be able to tie it back?
Name the requirement
Keeping enough length for a low ponytail matters to you.
Propose the longer outline
Shall we discuss a collarbone-length outline instead?
Keep the fringe separate
The photo has a straight fringe; is that part of your request?
Record the exclusion
No fringe is requested.
Confirm before cutting
Let us agree on the length before any cut begins.
Avoid copying everything
The photo is a reference, not permission for every feature.
Clarify front length
How much front length do you want retained?
Check the outline
Is this the shape you mean?
Avoid an absolute promise
I cannot promise every strand will tie back from the photo alone.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood the priority.
Read back the plan
Collarbone-length outline, retained ponytail length, no fringe.
Close the consultation point
That is the agreed direction, with no cut started yet.
6 language and precision notes
Reference versus instruction
A reference suggests features; the client must confirm which ones are intended.
At versus off
Length at the collarbone describes the endpoint; an amount off describes removal.
Fringe and bangs
These regional terms can refer to the same front feature.
Enough for
This expresses a practical requirement that needs checking against the actual hair.
Instead
Instead marks the agreed longer endpoint replacing the photo's shorter one.
No cut started
Clarification occurs before the irreversible action, leaving room to correct the plan.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which read-back preserves the agreement?

Answer and explanation

B. Collarbone-length outline, retain ponytail length, no fringe

The read-back includes the chosen length, practical requirement, and explicit fringe exclusion.

2. Which feature needs separate consent?

Answer and explanation

D. The straight fringe shown in the photo

A visible feature is not automatically requested merely because it appears in the reference.

3. What does trim fail to specify on its own?

Answer and explanation

B. The agreed amount or endpoint of length removal

Trim is imprecise unless the amount or resulting length is clarified.

4. Which promise is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

B. Every strand will definitely fit into a ponytail based only on this photo.

The reference does not establish how all of Alex's actual hair sections will tie back.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

Keep the ponytail length

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Alex: Client / Ren: Stylist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • length removal
  • chin-length
  • perimeter
  • read back
  • trim
  • retained length
  • collarbone-length
  • agreed direction
  • fringe
  • reference photo
  1. Alex
    I like the shape in this picture, but I still need to tie my hair back for work. Can we keep that?
  2. Ren
    Yes, let's talk through the 1 first. Is it the shape you like, or the length as well?
  3. Alex
    Mostly the shape. Looking at it now, the ends are much higher than mine. Where would they finish on me?
  4. Ren
    This one is 2. That could conflict with tying it back, so we should discuss a longer version.
  5. Alex
    Could it sit around here instead? I'm pointing to my collarbone. I don't want to spend the day pinning loose pieces back.
  6. Ren
    A 3 outline is the direction we'll discuss. I'll check your actual front and side lengths before promising what will reach.
  7. Alex
    Good. By outline, do you mean the edge around the bottom? That's the part of the picture I like.
  8. Ren
    Exactly. The 4 gives that outer shape. We can discuss the shape without copying how short the photograph is.
  9. Alex
    And please leave out the bit across the forehead. I don't want bangs. Are those the same as a fringe?
  10. Ren
    Yes, 5 is another word for bangs. I'll make that clear in the notes.
  11. Alex
    Thank you. I usually wear a low ponytail, not a high one. Does that help explain the length I need?
  12. Ren
    It does. The 6 needs to support how you wear it. A photo alone can't tell me whether every piece will reach.
  13. Alex
    I understand. I just don't want you to see the picture and assume I want everything in it.
  14. Ren
    No problem. I'll 7 the main points: longer outline at the collarbone, keep the ponytail requirement, and no bangs.
  15. Alex
    That's right. When people say just a trim, does that tell you how much to remove?
  16. Ren
    Not precisely. A 8 could mean different amounts to different clients. We still need to agree where the ends should sit.
  17. Alex
    Then let's agree on that before you start. I'd rather check the length in the mirror first.
  18. Ren
    Certainly. We can confirm the 9 before any cutting. Nothing has been cut yet.
  19. Alex
    Great. Longer than the picture, enough length to discuss tying it back, and definitely no fringe.
  20. Ren
    That's the 10. I'll check the remaining details with you before we begin.

Answers and explanations
  1. reference photo Turn 2. The photograph starts a discussion; it does not authorize copying every visible feature.
  2. chin-length Turn 4. The reference ends at the chin, which Alex has not chosen as the new endpoint.
  3. collarbone-length Turn 6. Collarbone length is the longer direction Alex chooses for discussion, not a guaranteed ponytail result.
  4. perimeter Turn 8. Perimeter means the outer edge defining the haircut, rather than every internal length.
  5. fringe Turn 10. Fringe and bangs name the front feature that Alex explicitly declines in this conversation.
  6. retained length Turn 12. Retained length is what stays, not the amount removed during the haircut.
  7. read back Turn 14. Reading back repeats the selected details so Alex can correct them before cutting.
  8. trim Turn 16. A trim suggests limited removal but does not specify an agreed amount or final endpoint.
  9. length removal Turn 18. Length removal means the amount cut off, distinct from where the finished ends will sit.
  10. agreed direction Turn 20. The agreed direction records the chosen outline and exclusions without inventing an exact cutting plan.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Alex: I like the shape in this picture, but I still need to tie my hair back for work. Can we keep that?
  2. Ren: Yes, let's talk through the reference photo first. Is it the shape you like, or the length as well?
  3. Alex: Mostly the shape. Looking at it now, the ends are much higher than mine. Where would they finish on me?
  4. Ren: This one is chin-length. That could conflict with tying it back, so we should discuss a longer version.
  5. Alex: Could it sit around here instead? I'm pointing to my collarbone. I don't want to spend the day pinning loose pieces back.
  6. Ren: A collarbone-length outline is the direction we'll discuss. I'll check your actual front and side lengths before promising what will reach.
  7. Alex: Good. By outline, do you mean the edge around the bottom? That's the part of the picture I like.
  8. Ren: Exactly. The perimeter gives that outer shape. We can discuss the shape without copying how short the photograph is.
  9. Alex: And please leave out the bit across the forehead. I don't want bangs. Are those the same as a fringe?
  10. Ren: Yes, fringe is another word for bangs. I'll make that clear in the notes.
  11. Alex: Thank you. I usually wear a low ponytail, not a high one. Does that help explain the length I need?
  12. Ren: It does. The retained length needs to support how you wear it. A photo alone can't tell me whether every piece will reach.
  13. Alex: I understand. I just don't want you to see the picture and assume I want everything in it.
  14. Ren: No problem. I'll read back the main points: longer outline at the collarbone, keep the ponytail requirement, and no bangs.
  15. Alex: That's right. When people say just a trim, does that tell you how much to remove?
  16. Ren: Not precisely. A trim could mean different amounts to different clients. We still need to agree where the ends should sit.
  17. Alex: Then let's agree on that before you start. I'd rather check the length in the mirror first.
  18. Ren: Certainly. We can confirm the length removal before any cutting. Nothing has been cut yet.
  19. Alex: Great. Longer than the picture, enough length to discuss tying it back, and definitely no fringe.
  20. Ren: That's the agreed direction. I'll check the remaining details with you before we begin.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A bob photograph with a ponytail requirement

A first consultation before any cutting.

  1. Stylist: What appeals to you about this reference photo: the length, the fringe, or the outline?
  2. Client: Mostly the neat outline. I still need to tie my hair back at work.
  3. Stylist: The pictured length ends at the chin. That conflicts with your low ponytail requirement.
  4. Client: I hadn't noticed how short it was. Could we keep it near my collarbone?
  5. Stylist: Let's discuss that longer perimeter. Are you also asking for hair across your forehead?
  6. Client: No fringe, please. I wear glasses, and I like keeping the front tucked away.
  7. Stylist: So the photograph guides the outline, not the length or the front section.
  8. Client: Exactly. Could you show me the proposed length in the mirror before starting?
  9. Stylist: Yes, here is the point we discussed. Does this match what you meant?
  10. Client: It does. Keep that length, and leave the front long enough to tuck.
What does a tidy-up include?

A barber clarifies a returning client's brief request.

  1. Barber: When you say a tidy-up today, which parts feel too long or untidy?
  2. Client: Around my ears, mainly. I'm growing the top out for a different style.
  3. Barber: Then shortening the top isn't part of your request. What about the neckline?
  4. Client: That needs attention too, but I don't want the whole cut much shorter.
  5. Barber: Understood. Your last appointment note says a short all-over cut; today's preference differs.
  6. Client: Yes, please don't use that old description. I've been growing it for weeks.
  7. Barber: I'll record that you're keeping the top and changing the outline around the ears.
  8. Client: Could you also check the back with me? I can't describe it very well.
  9. Barber: Certainly. We'll use the hand mirror to agree on the outline together first.
  10. Client: That helps. The top stays long, and we agree on the edges before starting.
Bangs and fringe mean the same area

A visitor uses a different regional word at the salon.

  1. Stylist: You mentioned bangs on the phone. Our booking note uses fringe; that's the same area.
  2. Client: Good, because I wondered whether I'd booked a completely different kind of haircut.
  3. Stylist: No, the words vary. What length do you have in mind across your forehead?
  4. Client: I want it below my eyebrows, not as short as the display photograph.
  5. Stylist: Thank you. Would you like the center and the sides discussed separately today?
  6. Client: Yes. I like longer pieces at the sides, but I don't know their name.
  7. Stylist: You don't need the technical name. Point to the areas you want to retain.
  8. Client: These pieces here. I usually tuck them behind my ears when I'm working.
  9. Stylist: I see. We'll keep those longer and confirm the center length in the mirror.
  10. Client: That's the distinction I was trying to explain. Please keep those side pieces.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Keep the ponytail length
  1. Read turns 1-10 in pairs. Stress chin-length, collarbone-length, and no fringe without changing the selected outline.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Read the question about trim and distinguish length retained from length removed.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Confirm another reference-photo request

A client shows an ear-length reference but agrees to a shoulder-length outline. The client wants front length retained and explicitly declines bangs. No cut has started.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Stylist: "The agreed outline is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. shoulder-length

Shoulder length is the confirmed direction, not the reference's shorter endpoint.

2. Client: "Please retain the ___ length."

Answer and explanation

C. front

Front length is the specific feature the client wants preserved.

3. Stylist: "No ___ are requested."

Answer and explanation

D. bangs

Bangs are explicitly declined despite any appearance in the reference.

4. Client: "The cut has not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

C. started

The brief places the clarification before any cutting begins.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Extend the vocabularyPractice the grammarRehearse with AI

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Language workshop: Keep the ponytail length
Communication goal: Separate a reference image into chosen and unchosen features, then confirm the agreed length before cutting.
Language guidance: The photo has a chin-length endpoint and a straight fringe. Alex chooses neither automatically. The agreed outline discussion is collarbone-length, with no fringe requested and retained ponytail length as the practical requirement.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Which features of this photograph do you like? / Do you want the outline, the length, or both? / The photograph shows a chin-length bob. / Do you need to be able to tie it back? / Keeping enough length for a low ponytail matters to you. / Shall we discuss a collarbone-length outline instead? / The photo has a straight fringe; is that part of your request? / No fringe is requested. / Let us agree on the length before any cut begins. / The photo is a reference, not permission for every feature. / How much front length do you want retained? / Is this the shape you mean? / I cannot promise every strand will tie back from the photo alone. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood the priority. / Collarbone-length outline, retained ponytail length, no fringe. / That is the agreed direction, with no cut started yet.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Communication goal: Separate a reference image into chosen and unchosen features, then confirm the agreed length before cutting.
Language guidance: The photo has a chin-length endpoint and a straight fringe. Alex chooses neither automatically. The agreed outline discussion is collarbone-length, with no fringe requested and retained ponytail length as the practical requirement.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Which features of this photograph do you like? / Do you want the outline, the length, or both? / The photograph shows a chin-length bob. / Do you need to be able to tie it back? / Keeping enough length for a low ponytail matters to you. / Shall we discuss a collarbone-length outline instead? / The photo has a straight fringe; is that part of your request? / No fringe is requested. / Let us agree on the length before any cut begins. / The photo is a reference, not permission for every feature. / How much front length do you want retained? / Is this the shape you mean? / I cannot promise every strand will tie back from the photo alone. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood the priority. / Collarbone-length outline, retained ponytail length, no fringe. / That is the agreed direction, with no cut started yet.
Vocabulary: consultation: Discussion to establish preferences, suitability, and the proposed service. / reference photo: Image used to communicate an idea, not automatically a complete instruction. / bob: Short-to-medium haircut family with a defined outline, varying in length and shape. / outline: Outer visible shape or boundary of the haircut. / perimeter: Outer edge defining the haircut's overall length and shape. / chin length: Length described relative to the chin. / collarbone length: Length described relative to the collarbone. / retained length: Hair length kept rather than removed. / low ponytail: Hair gathered and secured low at the back of the head. / fringe: Front hair shaped to fall over or around the forehead; also called bangs. / bangs: Common US term for a fringe. / straight fringe: Fringe with a visually straight outline across the front. / face-framing: Hair shaped around the face as a distinct design feature. / front length: Length retained in the hair toward the face. / side length: Length of hair at the sides of the head. / back length: Length of hair at the back of the head. / parting: Line or division where hair is separated; also called a part. / blunt outline: Perimeter with a visually solid, even edge. / layers: Hair lengths arranged at different levels within a haircut. / trim: Removal of a limited amount, which still needs an agreed length. / length removal: Amount of hair to be cut off. / shrinkage: Shorter visible length when curls recoil from a stretched or wet state. / agreed plan: Service details explicitly confirmed with the client. / consent: Permission for the specified action, not every possible variation.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10 in pairs. Stress chin-length, collarbone-length, and no fringe without changing the selected outline. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Read the question about trim and distinguish length retained from length removed. Complete the shoulder-length transfer, check the key, then read it with the shorter reference and longer chosen endpoint clearly contrasted.
Partner: Alex: Client / Ren: Stylist
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A client shows an ear-length reference but agrees to a shoulder-length outline. The client wants front length retained and explicitly declines bangs. No cut has started.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Alex: Then let's agree on that before you start. I'd rather check the length in the mirror first.
Ren: Certainly. We can confirm the length removal before any cutting. Nothing has been cut yet.
Alex: Great. Longer than the picture, enough length to discuss tying it back, and definitely no fringe.
Ren: That's the agreed direction. I'll check the remaining details with you before we begin.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Professional audience: Hairstylists, barbers, salon assistants, and salon reception staff

Lesson 01: Turning a reference photo into a clear request
Original fictional case: Client Alex shows stylist Ren a photograph of a chin-length bob with a straight fringe. Alex likes the outline but wants enough length for a low ponytail. No cut has started. Through clarification, they agree to discuss a collarbone-length outline rather than copying the chin-length endpoint. Alex confirms that no fringe is requested. Ren must keep the visual reference, practical length requirement, and agreed plan distinct. 02Describing shape, texture, and everyday stylingLess bulk, not bare sides+

Your goal

Compare visible features, clarify an everyday styling limit, and distinguish a style label from an agreed result.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

Less bulk, not bare sides

Client Morgan wants less fullness at the neckline but no exposed skin at the sides. Barber Dev has two reference photographs. Photo A, labelled low taper, shows visible hair covering the sides. Photo B, labelled skin fade, shows exposed skin at the shortest area of the sides. Morgan spends five minutes styling each morning. Dev compares only these supplied appearances. No cutting method, exact length, or guaranteed maintenance time has been agreed. Morgan prefers the covered-side appearance of A, while keeping the neckline concern separate.

Morgan: Client / Dev: Barber

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does Morgan want at the neckline?

Answer and explanation

C. Less fullness

Morgan asks for reduced neckline fullness while separately declining exposed sides.

2. Which supplied photo shows covered sides?

Answer and explanation

B. Photo A

Photo A is described as retaining visible hair over the sides.

3. How much daily styling time does Morgan report?

Answer and explanation

B. Five minutes

The brief states five minutes as Morgan's current morning styling allowance.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Photo A and photo B are defined by their supplied appearances. A keeps visible hair over the sides; B exposes skin at the shortest area. Morgan chooses the covered-side appearance, not every feature associated with the label.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
fullness
Visual amount of hair or volume in an area.reduce neckline fullness
bulk
Perceived density or weight in a section of hair.clarify unwanted bulk
neckline
Lower outline of the hair at the back of the neck.discuss the neckline
nape
Back portion of the neck near the lower hairline.identify the nape
taper
Gradual change in hair length; the intended appearance still needs clarification.discuss a taper
low taper
Style label suggesting a low-positioned transition, interpreted with a reference.clarify the low taper
fade
Gradual transition between shorter and longer hair lengths.compare fade references
skin fade
Fade whose shortest area exposes skin in the supplied reference.identify the skin fade
transition
Visible change from one length or appearance to another.describe the transition
blend
Visual merging of different hair lengths or areas.discuss the blend
exposed skin
Skin visible where the supplied reference has very short hair.avoid exposed skin
coverage
Visible hair remaining over an area.retain side coverage
sideburn
Hair extending down in front of the ear.clarify sideburn length
crown
Area toward the upper back of the head.identify the crown
hairline
Boundary where hair growth meets visible skin.describe the hairline
natural texture
Hair's own visible or tactile pattern and behavior.discuss natural texture
density
Amount of hair growing within an area.assess hair density
strand thickness
Thickness of an individual hair, distinct from overall density.distinguish strand thickness
growth pattern
Direction or arrangement in which hair naturally grows.discuss the growth pattern
cowlick
Area where growth direction makes hair stand or turn differently.identify a cowlick
finish
Final styled appearance following the service.compare the finish
styling routine
Usual steps and time used to arrange hair.describe the styling routine
upkeep
Ongoing effort or appointments needed to maintain an appearance.discuss upkeep
guide comb
Clipper attachment marked with a nominal remaining hair length; numbering depends on the tool.check the guide-comb length

Hair density concerns the amount of hair in an area; strand thickness concerns individual hairs. Neither a photo nor a style name establishes Morgan's actual hair characteristics, suitable cutting method, or guaranteed daily maintenance time.

C Notice the language

Compare what both people can see

Style names can vary between shops, images, and clients. Anchor the comparison in visible features rather than insisting that one label settles the request. A daily time limit expresses a preference; it does not prove a proposed style will meet it.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the priority
You want less fullness at the neckline.
Keep the exclusion clear
You do not want exposed skin at the sides.
Compare the references
Photo A keeps visible hair over the sides.
Describe the contrast
Photo B exposes skin in its shortest area.
Check the label
When you say taper, is this the appearance you mean?
Separate the areas
Let us discuss the neckline separately from the sides.
Ask about routine
How much time do you usually spend styling it?
Record the time limit
You have about five minutes each morning.
Avoid a guarantee
I cannot promise that routine from the photograph alone.
Clarify coverage
Is keeping the sides covered the important feature?
Avoid technical assumptions
We have not agreed on a cutting method yet.
Ask for a visible comparison
Which of these side appearances is closer to your preference?
Clarify bulk
Where does the fullness bother you most?
Check the natural pattern
We still need to assess how your hair grows.
Read back the appearance
Less neckline fullness, with visible hair covering the sides.
Confirm the boundary
Choosing A does not mean copying every detail.
6 language and precision notes
Less versus none
Less fullness does not mean removing all hair from an area.
At versus on
At the neckline identifies a boundary; on the sides identifies a broader area.
Covered versus exposed
These describe the visible difference relevant to this client's preference.
Label versus reference
A style label helps discussion but does not replace an agreed visual description.
Time budget
Five minutes states available effort, not a verified styling outcome.
Separate priorities
Neckline fullness and side coverage can be discussed without treating them as one decision.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which comparison is supported?

Answer and explanation

D. A shows covered sides; B shows exposed skin at the shortest area.

Only the supplied visible difference is established by the two photographs.

2. Which clarification best follows less bulk?

Answer and explanation

D. Where does the fullness bother you most?

The location question clarifies the requested change without inventing removal or exposure.

3. What remains unagreed?

Answer and explanation

D. The cutting method and exact length

The brief explicitly leaves the method and exact length unagreed.

4. Which summary preserves both preferences?

Answer and explanation

A. Reduce neckline fullness while retaining visible side coverage.

The correct summary keeps the desired reduction and the coverage boundary distinct.

Answers and explanations
  1. neckline Turn 2. Neckline identifies the lower boundary at the back where Morgan wants less fullness, separate from the side-coverage preference.
  2. coverage Turn 4. Coverage means hair remains visibly over the sides, which matches Morgan's stated preference in this comparison.
  3. Exposed skin Turn 6. Exposed skin is the feature Morgan declines; reducing fullness at the neckline does not authorize adding it at the sides.
  4. visual comparison Turn 8. Visual comparison anchors the discussion in observable features rather than assuming a style label has one identical interpretation everywhere.
  5. bulk Turn 10. Bulk refers to perceived fullness or weight in an area, here specifically near the collar rather than across the entire head.
  6. styling routine Turn 12. Styling routine describes the time and effort Morgan normally uses, which informs consultation without guaranteeing a particular result.
  7. upkeep Turn 14. Upkeep covers ongoing effort needed to maintain an appearance; it remains a discussion rather than an established five-minute guarantee.
  8. growth pattern Turn 16. Growth pattern is the direction or arrangement of natural hair growth, which must be assessed on the client rather than inferred from another person's photo.
  9. appearance Turn 18. Appearance describes the visible direction being discussed, without claiming an exact length or technical cutting method has already been agreed.
  10. time budget Turn 20. Time budget records Morgan's available styling time, not a promise that the proposed appearance will always take exactly five minutes.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Covered sides or a skin-level finish?

A barber compares two reference images with a client.

  1. Barber: These photographs show different side lengths. Which feature matters more to you today?
  2. Client: I want the neckline less bulky, but I don't want skin showing at the sides.
  3. Barber: In this taper photograph, the sides keep more coverage than in the skin fade.
  4. Client: Then the taper picture sounds closer. Does its name tell you exactly what I want?
  5. Barber: Not completely. We still need to agree on the visible length and overall shape.
  6. Client: I also have only five minutes in the morning, so elaborate styling won't suit me.
  7. Barber: How do you normally wear the top: brushed back, to the side, or natural?
  8. Client: Usually to the side with very little product. I want to keep doing that.
  9. Barber: We'll use the covered sides as our reference and discuss the top around that routine.
  10. Client: Yes, that comparison helps more than just choosing a haircut name from the menu.
Layers without losing the outer length

A salon consultation separates internal shape from the outside outline.

  1. Stylist: You asked about layers but also said the overall length must stay the same.
  2. Client: Right. I thought layers always meant making the whole haircut much shorter than it is.
  3. Stylist: Layers concern different lengths within the shape; the outside outline is a separate choice.
  4. Client: Can I have more movement without making it shorter overall?
  5. Stylist: Yes. Which reference image shows the kind of movement you're describing around the front?
  6. Client: This one does, though I don't like the very short pieces near the face.
  7. Stylist: That's important. We can discuss the longer sections without copying those shorter front pieces.
  8. Client: Please show me which parts you mean. I'm better with pictures than technical descriptions.
  9. Stylist: Here and here. Does that match your request to keep the outer length visible?
  10. Client: Yes, keep the overall length. Show me where the shorter layers would be.
The finish should reflect the usual curls

A client asks about a style photographed on straightened hair.

  1. Stylist: The reference photograph shows a smooth finish. Do you usually wear your hair straight?
  2. Client: No, I wear my natural curls almost every day and rarely spend time straightening them.
  3. Stylist: Then we should discuss how you want the shape to look with your usual texture.
  4. Client: I like the photograph's outline, but I wasn't asking for that exact straight finish.
  5. Stylist: Thank you for clarifying. Would seeing a curly reference help us discuss the same outline?
  6. Client: Yes, particularly at the sides. That's where I want the shape to feel less wide.
  7. Stylist: This second picture may be a better discussion point, although your result won't be identical.
  8. Client: I understand. I want a clear idea, not a promise that my curls will match.
  9. Stylist: Tell me what you like in this one, and we'll agree on those features.
  10. Client: The softer sides and retained top length are the features I'd like to discuss.

Lesson 02: Describing shape, texture, and everyday styling
Original fictional case: Client Morgan wants less fullness at the neckline but no exposed skin at the sides. Barber Dev has two reference photographs. Photo A, labelled low taper, shows visible hair covering the sides. Photo B, labelled skin fade, shows exposed skin at the shortest area of the sides. Morgan spends five minutes styling each morning. Dev compares only these supplied appearances. No cutting method, exact length, or guaranteed maintenance time has been agreed. Morgan prefers the covered-side appearance of A, while keeping the neckline concern separate.
Language workshop: Less bulk, not bare sides
Communication goal: Compare visible features, clarify an everyday styling limit, and distinguish a style label from an agreed result.
Language guidance: Photo A and photo B are defined by their supplied appearances. A keeps visible hair over the sides; B exposes skin at the shortest area. Morgan chooses the covered-side appearance, not every feature associated with the label.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): You want less fullness at the neckline. / You do not want exposed skin at the sides. / Photo A keeps visible hair over the sides. / Photo B exposes skin in its shortest area. / When you say taper, is this the appearance you mean? / Let us discuss the neckline separately from the sides. / How much time do you usually spend styling it? / You have about five minutes each morning. / I cannot promise that routine from the photograph alone. / Is keeping the sides covered the important feature? / We have not agreed on a cutting method yet. / Which of these side appearances is closer to your preference? / Where does the fullness bother you most? / We still need to assess how your hair grows. / Less neckline fullness, with visible hair covering the sides. / Choosing A does not mean copying every detail.
Vocabulary: fullness: Visual amount of hair or volume in an area. / bulk: Perceived density or weight in a section of hair. / neckline: Lower outline of the hair at the back of the neck. / nape: Back portion of the neck near the lower hairline. / taper: Gradual change in hair length; the intended appearance still needs clarification. / low taper: Style label suggesting a low-positioned transition, interpreted with a reference. / fade: Gradual transition between shorter and longer hair lengths. / skin fade: Fade whose shortest area exposes skin in the supplied reference. / transition: Visible change from one length or appearance to another. / blend: Visual merging of different hair lengths or areas. / exposed skin: Skin visible where the supplied reference has very short hair. / coverage: Visible hair remaining over an area. / sideburn: Hair extending down in front of the ear. / crown: Area toward the upper back of the head. / hairline: Boundary where hair growth meets visible skin. / natural texture: Hair's own visible or tactile pattern and behavior. / density: Amount of hair growing within an area. / strand thickness: Thickness of an individual hair, distinct from overall density. / growth pattern: Direction or arrangement in which hair naturally grows. / cowlick: Area where growth direction makes hair stand or turn differently. / finish: Final styled appearance following the service. / styling routine: Usual steps and time used to arrange hair. / upkeep: Ongoing effort or appointments needed to maintain an appearance. / guide comb: Clipper attachment marked with a nominal remaining hair length; numbering depends on the tool.
Speaking task: Read the photo comparison. Stress covered sides for A and exposed skin for B; keep the neckline request separate. Switch roles and read the five-minute routine exchange without turning the client's time budget into a guarantee. Complete and check the appearance transfer; read the exclusion as clearly as the requested change.
Partner: Morgan: Client / Dev: Barber
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Photo C shows hair covering the sides. Photo D shows exposed skin at the shortest side area. The client chooses C, dislikes fullness at the nape, and has ten minutes for daily styling. No method is agreed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Morgan: Let me check the main point: I prefer A's covered sides, and I want less fullness at the neckline near my collar.
Dev: Yes. That is the agreed appearance for discussion. It does not yet specify an exact length or technique, and it does not include the exposed side area in B.
Morgan: Yes. Show me how short you mean before we start, especially around the ears.
Dev: We will confirm the remaining details before proceeding. Your five-minute time budget stays in the consultation, alongside the neckline priority and the clear preference for visible hair over the sides.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

03Discussing color without promising a photographA cool tone by Friday?+

Your goal

Clarify a color goal and previous-product uncertainty while explaining what a consultation has not yet established.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

A cool tone by Friday?

Client Priya wants the cool color tone in a photograph before Friday. Colorist Ellis is conducting a consultation only today. Priya used a home-color product previously but cannot identify it. Suitability, service duration, and cost have not been assessed, and no color appointment is booked. Ellis distinguishes the desired tonal character from the lightness or darkness of the reference. The conversation records the unknown product and the deadline without promising a formula, exact photographic match, or completion by Friday.

Priya: Client / Ellis: Colorist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is booked today?

Answer and explanation

B. A consultation only

Today's booking is explicitly a consultation, not a color-service appointment.

2. Which history detail remains unknown?

Answer and explanation

A. The previous home-color product

Priya cannot identify the earlier product, so its identity remains unknown.

3. Which claim is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

B. The photographed result will be completed by Friday.

Neither suitability nor time has been established to support that deadline promise.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Level describes lightness or darkness; tone describes color character. A cool reference tone does not identify a suitable product, establish a formula, or show that the same result can be achieved on Priya's hair by Friday.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
color consultation
Discussion and assessment before a possible color service.book a color consultation
color goal
Desired color appearance, subject to assessment.clarify the color goal
level
Relative lightness or darkness of hair color.discuss the color level
tone
Color character such as warm or cool, distinct from lightness.clarify the tone
cool tone
Tonal family associated with cooler color character.identify a cool tone
warm tone
Tonal family associated with warmer color character.describe a warm tone
ash
Salon term for a cool tonal direction; naming systems vary.discuss an ash tone
golden
Term describing a warm gold tonal character.identify golden tones
base color
Main underlying or overall color being discussed.clarify the base color
regrowth
Hair grown since a previous color service.discuss visible regrowth
color history
Record of previous coloring products and services.review color history
home color
Hair-color product applied outside the salon setting.identify previous home color
strand test
Assessment on a small hair sample to examine color response; not a skin allergy test.review the strand-test result
permanent color
Product category designed for lasting color change.distinguish permanent color
demi-permanent color
Color category distinct from permanent and temporary products; details vary.clarify demi-permanent color
semi-permanent color
Color category generally intended to fade over washes; details vary.discuss semi-permanent color
temporary color
Product category intended for short-lived color effects.identify temporary color
lightener
Product category used to lighten hair, requiring professional assessment.discuss a lightener service
toner
Product or service used to adjust tonal character.clarify a toner service
highlights
Selected areas made visibly lighter than surrounding hair.discuss highlights
balayage
Term associated with hand-painted color placement.clarify balayage expectations
banding
Visibly distinct bands of color along the hair that need assessment.describe the color banding
suitability
Whether a proposed service is appropriate after assessment.assess service suitability
service estimate
Provisional statement of expected time or cost.provide a service estimate

Unknown is a useful, accurate history entry. Do not relabel an unidentified home product as temporary or harmless. Product names, instructions, warnings, professional assessment, and relevant checks must be handled through actual salon procedures.

C Notice the language

Translate the goal without selling certainty

A client may use brighter, lighter, warmer, and fresher interchangeably. Clarify the visible quality they mean before using technical language. A deadline can guide planning, but it cannot replace assessment of the service, product history, time, and cost.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Clarify the attraction
Is it the cool tone or the lighter level that you like?
Explain level
Level describes how light or dark the color is.
Explain tone
Tone describes the color character, such as warm or cool.
Set today's scope
Today is a consultation only.
Ask about history
Which home-color product did you use?
Record uncertainty
The previous product is not yet identified.
Avoid guessing a category
I cannot assume it was temporary color.
Acknowledge the deadline
I understand you would prefer it before Friday.
Separate goal and promise
The photo shows your goal, not a confirmed result.
Name the pending assessment
Suitability has not yet been assessed.
Keep time provisional
We have not established the service duration.
Keep price provisional
We have not assessed the cost yet.
Clarify booking status
No color appointment is booked.
Avoid a formula promise
We have not selected a product or formula.
Confirm the next discussion
We need the relevant history before confirming the service plan.
Summarize accurately
Cool-tone goal, unknown previous product, consultation only.
6 language and precision notes
By versus before
By includes the deadline; before means earlier than it. Neither establishes the salon can meet the request.
Lighter versus cooler
Lighter concerns level, while cooler concerns tonal character.
Not yet
This marks an unfinished assessment rather than a permanent refusal.
Unknown versus none
Unknown previous product does not mean no previous coloring occurred.
Goal versus result
A desired appearance is not evidence of an achievable exact match.
Estimate versus quote
An estimate is provisional; use the salon's actual terms for confirmed prices.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which explanation distinguishes level and tone?

Answer and explanation

D. Level is lightness or darkness; tone is color character.

The correct explanation separates two different dimensions of color description.

2. Which history entry is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. Previous home-color product not identified.

The client reports prior use but cannot identify the product or category.

3. Which response handles the deadline accurately?

Answer and explanation

A. I understand Friday matters; we have not assessed whether that is achievable.

The response acknowledges the preference without replacing the pending assessment with a promise.

4. Which status should reception receive?

Answer and explanation

A. Consultation only; no color appointment booked.

The supplied facts establish only the consultation and an unbooked future service.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

A cool tone by Friday?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Priya: Client / Ellis: Colorist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • service duration
  • product identity
  • color goal
  • level
  • Tone
  • home color
  • cost
  • booking status
  • color consultation
  • Suitability
  1. Priya
    I love the cool color in this picture. I have an event on Friday, and I was hoping I could have it done before then.
  2. Ellis
    I understand the timing. Today is a 1, so we will clarify the goal and the relevant history before confirming what service might be appropriate.
  3. Priya
    Is cool the right word? I like that it does not look golden. I am less certain about whether I want it lighter.
  4. Ellis
    That helps. 2 describes that color character. We can discuss the cool quality separately from how light or dark you want the overall result.
  5. Priya
    I see. I like the cool look, but I don't necessarily want to go much lighter than I am now.
  6. Ellis
    Exactly. The 3 is the lightness or darkness. The picture gives us a starting point, but those two preferences still need their own discussion.
  7. Priya
    I used a color from a shop at home a while ago. I do not remember the name or what type it was.
  8. Ellis
    I will record the previous 4 as unidentified. We should not assume it was temporary, or say there was no previous color, simply because the name is missing.
  9. Priya
    I may have the packaging somewhere. Would the exact name be more useful than my memory of the color on the box?
  10. Ellis
    Yes, accurate 5 is useful for the assessment. For now, the honest record is that you used a product but cannot identify it.
  11. Priya
    Could you promise it for Friday, or should I make other plans for the event?
  12. Ellis
    Correct. 6 has not been assessed, and the photograph does not establish how your hair will respond. I cannot promise that exact result or deadline.
  13. Priya
    I appreciate knowing that before I plan around it. I also need to understand how much time I would have to set aside.
  14. Ellis
    We have not established the 7 yet. It depends on the plan after assessment, so I do not want you to treat a guessed length of appointment as confirmed.
  15. Priya
    And the price? I have seen very different prices for color services, and I do not know which description applies to my request.
  16. Ellis
    The 8 has not been assessed either. We need to clarify the proposed service before discussing a meaningful amount, rather than selecting a price from the photograph alone.
  17. Priya
    Okay, let's work out the plan first. I'll try to find the old packaging before we talk about booking.
  18. Ellis
    I will record your 9 and the unknown product history. No product or formula has been selected, and no color appointment is booked.
  19. Priya
    Please keep the cool-tone photo with my notes. I don't want the Friday deadline to rush the decision.
  20. Ellis
    Exactly. The 10 remains consultation only. We will keep the deadline, tonal preference, product uncertainty, and pending time and cost assessment separate in the record.

Answers and explanations
  1. color consultation Turn 2. Color consultation identifies today's booked discussion and assessment, not an appointment to perform the requested color service.
  2. Tone Turn 4. Tone describes the warm or cool character Priya likes, rather than the separate question of how light the color is.
  3. level Turn 6. Level refers to relative lightness or darkness, which remains a separate preference to clarify alongside the cool tone.
  4. home color Turn 8. Home color identifies the reported earlier product use without inventing its exact brand, category, or effects.
  5. product identity Turn 10. Product identity means the actual identifying information, which is more useful than guessing the category from a remembered package color.
  6. Suitability Turn 12. Suitability is whether the proposed service is appropriate after assessment; it has not been established by the reference or deadline.
  7. service duration Turn 14. Service duration is the time needed for the proposed service, which remains unassessed rather than guaranteed to fit before Friday.
  8. cost Turn 16. Cost has not been assessed because the service plan is not established; no quoted total is available from the supplied facts.
  9. color goal Turn 18. Color goal captures the desired cool-tone appearance without turning it into a selected formula, accepted price, or booked service.
  10. booking status Turn 20. Booking status distinguishes today's consultation from a future color appointment, which remains unbooked despite the client's preferred deadline.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Priya: I love the cool color in this picture. I have an event on Friday, and I was hoping I could have it done before then.
  2. Ellis: I understand the timing. Today is a color consultation, so we will clarify the goal and the relevant history before confirming what service might be appropriate.
  3. Priya: Is cool the right word? I like that it does not look golden. I am less certain about whether I want it lighter.
  4. Ellis: That helps. Tone describes that color character. We can discuss the cool quality separately from how light or dark you want the overall result.
  5. Priya: I see. I like the cool look, but I don't necessarily want to go much lighter than I am now.
  6. Ellis: Exactly. The level is the lightness or darkness. The picture gives us a starting point, but those two preferences still need their own discussion.
  7. Priya: I used a color from a shop at home a while ago. I do not remember the name or what type it was.
  8. Ellis: I will record the previous home color as unidentified. We should not assume it was temporary, or say there was no previous color, simply because the name is missing.
  9. Priya: I may have the packaging somewhere. Would the exact name be more useful than my memory of the color on the box?
  10. Ellis: Yes, accurate product identity is useful for the assessment. For now, the honest record is that you used a product but cannot identify it.
  11. Priya: Could you promise it for Friday, or should I make other plans for the event?
  12. Ellis: Correct. Suitability has not been assessed, and the photograph does not establish how your hair will respond. I cannot promise that exact result or deadline.
  13. Priya: I appreciate knowing that before I plan around it. I also need to understand how much time I would have to set aside.
  14. Ellis: We have not established the service duration yet. It depends on the plan after assessment, so I do not want you to treat a guessed length of appointment as confirmed.
  15. Priya: And the price? I have seen very different prices for color services, and I do not know which description applies to my request.
  16. Ellis: The cost has not been assessed either. We need to clarify the proposed service before discussing a meaningful amount, rather than selecting a price from the photograph alone.
  17. Priya: Okay, let's work out the plan first. I'll try to find the old packaging before we talk about booking.
  18. Ellis: I will record your color goal and the unknown product history. No product or formula has been selected, and no color appointment is booked.
  19. Priya: Please keep the cool-tone photo with my notes. I don't want the Friday deadline to rush the decision.
  20. Ellis: Exactly. The booking status remains consultation only. We will keep the deadline, tonal preference, product uncertainty, and pending time and cost assessment separate in the record.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A Friday target is not a confirmed result

A client attends a color consultation.

  1. Stylist: Your photo shows a cool tone. Is that what you want, rather than extra lightness?
  2. Client: Yes, especially before Friday. Can I book exactly that result for the party?
  3. Stylist: First we need your color history. Do you know which home product you used?
  4. Client: I remember the box being red, but I don't remember the product name.
  5. Stylist: That leaves an important detail unknown. The photo can't confirm what is suitable here.
  6. Client: I thought today's consultation included the color service. Was that my mistake when booking?
  7. Stylist: The booking is for discussion only. We haven't assessed the service time or cost.
  8. Client: I'll look for the packaging when I get home. Can we still discuss the target?
  9. Stylist: Certainly. We can identify your preferred tone without promising a particular result by Friday.
  10. Client: Then let's do that. I won't treat this consultation as a confirmed color appointment.
Warmth is not the same as darkness

A client compares two salon shade samples.

  1. Stylist: You called both samples dark, but pointed to the golden one as your concern.
  2. Client: I struggle to describe it. The other sample looks calmer, even at similar depth.
  3. Stylist: Are you referring to the warm golden tone rather than how light it is?
  4. Client: Yes, that's closer. I don't necessarily want my hair made lighter this time.
  5. Stylist: Then we can discuss a less warm target without assuming a change in lightness.
  6. Client: Would calling it ash guarantee the exact shade on this sample ring for me?
  7. Stylist: No. The sample helps identify a preference; your own result needs an individual assessment.
  8. Client: That makes sense. Could you keep these two ideas separate in the consultation note?
  9. Stylist: Yes: less warmth requested, with no automatic request for lighter hair or new highlights.
  10. Client: That's the wording I needed. It avoids making the change sound larger than intended.
Remembering the missing appointment history

A stylist asks a returning client about an incomplete record.

  1. Stylist: Our note lists your last color visit. Is today's request about regrowth or the colored lengths?
  2. Client: The regrowth near my roots. I also used temporary color at home before my holiday.
  3. Stylist: Please include it in your history without guessing how it affects today's options.
  4. Client: I don't know the name, but my sister ordered it and may have the receipt.
  5. Stylist: That could help identify it. Shall we leave that part marked as unknown for now?
  6. Client: Yes. Please don't record that I haven't used anything just because I can't remember.
  7. Stylist: I'll state that a home product was used and its name remains unconfirmed.
  8. Client: Could we discuss haircut options today while the color question is still open?
  9. Stylist: We can discuss them separately. That doesn't decide whether a color service is suitable.
  10. Client: Good. I'll ask for the receipt, and today's conversation won't erase that missing detail.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A cool tone by Friday?
  1. Read the level-and-tone exchange twice, switching roles. Emphasize lighter versus cooler.
  2. Read the history and booking exchange. Keep unidentified product, consultation only, and unassessed time and cost unchanged.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Keep another color request provisional

A client requests a warmer tone for a future event. The previous product is unidentified. Today is consultation only, and neither duration nor price is assessed. No future service is booked.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Colorist: "The requested tonal direction is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. warmer

Warmer describes the desired color character in this new request.

2. Client: "My previous product remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unidentified

The product was used but its identity is still unknown.

3. Colorist: "Today is a ___ only."

Answer and explanation

C. consultation

The appointment covers discussion rather than an approved color service.

4. Reception: "No future service is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. booked

A desired result and event do not create a confirmed appointment.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 03: Discussing color without promising a photograph
Original fictional case: Client Priya wants the cool color tone in a photograph before Friday. Colorist Ellis is conducting a consultation only today. Priya used a home-color product previously but cannot identify it. Suitability, service duration, and cost have not been assessed, and no color appointment is booked. Ellis distinguishes the desired tonal character from the lightness or darkness of the reference. The conversation records the unknown product and the deadline without promising a formula, exact photographic match, or completion by Friday.
Language workshop: A cool tone by Friday?
Communication goal: Clarify a color goal and previous-product uncertainty while explaining what a consultation has not yet established.
Language guidance: Level describes lightness or darkness; tone describes color character. A cool reference tone does not identify a suitable product, establish a formula, or show that the same result can be achieved on Priya's hair by Friday.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Is it the cool tone or the lighter level that you like? / Level describes how light or dark the color is. / Tone describes the color character, such as warm or cool. / Today is a consultation only. / Which home-color product did you use? / The previous product is not yet identified. / I cannot assume it was temporary color. / I understand you would prefer it before Friday. / The photo shows your goal, not a confirmed result. / Suitability has not yet been assessed. / We have not established the service duration. / We have not assessed the cost yet. / No color appointment is booked. / We have not selected a product or formula. / We need the relevant history before confirming the service plan. / Cool-tone goal, unknown previous product, consultation only.
Vocabulary: color consultation: Discussion and assessment before a possible color service. / color goal: Desired color appearance, subject to assessment. / level: Relative lightness or darkness of hair color. / tone: Color character such as warm or cool, distinct from lightness. / cool tone: Tonal family associated with cooler color character. / warm tone: Tonal family associated with warmer color character. / ash: Salon term for a cool tonal direction; naming systems vary. / golden: Term describing a warm gold tonal character. / base color: Main underlying or overall color being discussed. / regrowth: Hair grown since a previous color service. / color history: Record of previous coloring products and services. / home color: Hair-color product applied outside the salon setting. / strand test: Assessment on a small hair sample to examine color response; not a skin allergy test. / permanent color: Product category designed for lasting color change. / demi-permanent color: Color category distinct from permanent and temporary products; details vary. / semi-permanent color: Color category generally intended to fade over washes; details vary. / temporary color: Product category intended for short-lived color effects. / lightener: Product category used to lighten hair, requiring professional assessment. / toner: Product or service used to adjust tonal character. / highlights: Selected areas made visibly lighter than surrounding hair. / balayage: Term associated with hand-painted color placement. / banding: Visibly distinct bands of color along the hair that need assessment. / suitability: Whether a proposed service is appropriate after assessment. / service estimate: Provisional statement of expected time or cost.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
