04Booking time and handling appointment changesThe appointment no longer fits+

Your goal

Explain a delay, calculate the earliest ordinary finish, and offer a reschedule without promising a shortened service.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

The appointment no longer fits

Receptionist Hana tells client Luis that the 2:00 haircut appointment is running twenty minutes late. The usual service duration is forty minutes, while Luis must leave by 2:45. A 2:20 start would ordinarily finish at 3:00, so the appointment no longer fits the departure requirement. A 10:00 appointment tomorrow is available. This fictional salon waives the change fee when its own delay causes this rescheduling. Luis chooses tomorrow at 10:00, and Hana confirms the replacement appointment without keeping today as an active second booking.

Luis: Client / Hana: Receptionist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the revised start based on the stated delay?

Answer and explanation

D. 2:20

Twenty minutes after the original 2:00 appointment gives a 2:20 start.

2. When would the ordinary forty-minute service finish?

Answer and explanation

A. 3:00

Adding forty minutes to 2:20 gives an ordinary finish of 3:00.

3. Which replacement does Luis choose?

Answer and explanation

C. Tomorrow at 10:00

The client chooses the available 10:00 appointment tomorrow as the replacement.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The delay changes the start, not the service duration. A 2:20 start plus forty minutes gives 3:00, fifteen minutes after the client must leave. Do not imply the ordinary service can simply fit into twenty-five minutes.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
appointment slot
Specific time reserved for a service.confirm the appointment slot
scheduled start
Originally planned beginning time.state the scheduled start
delay
Difference between the planned and later expected start.explain the delay
revised start
Updated beginning time following a change.confirm the revised start
service duration
Amount of time normally allocated to the service.state the service duration
estimated finish
Expected ending time based on available information.calculate the estimated finish
departure deadline
Latest time the client must leave.respect the departure deadline
time conflict
Incompatibility between two timing requirements.identify the time conflict
availability
Whether a slot can currently be offered.check tomorrow's availability
reschedule
Move an appointment to a different time.reschedule the haircut
replacement booking
New appointment taking the place of the original.confirm the replacement booking
cancellation
Removal of an appointment from the schedule.record the cancellation
change fee
Charge associated with altering a booking under a stated policy.explain the change fee
fee waiver
Decision not to charge an otherwise applicable fee.apply the fee waiver
salon-caused delay
Delay originating from the salon's schedule or operations.acknowledge the salon-caused delay
late arrival
Client reaching the appointment after its scheduled start.distinguish a late arrival
no-show
Failure to attend without the required notice under a local policy.clarify the no-show category
notice period
Required advance time for a change under a specified policy.explain the notice period
confirmation message
Written or spoken statement of agreed booking details.send a confirmation message
booking record
Stored appointment details and status.update the booking record
duplicate booking
Two appointments unintentionally active for the same intended service.avoid a duplicate booking
waitlist
List of clients seeking a slot if one becomes available.explain the waitlist
service allocation
Time assigned to carry out the planned service.preserve the service allocation
calendar date
Specific day identified by date, not only a relative term.confirm the calendar date

This salon waives the change fee because its delay caused the rescheduling. That is a supplied local policy, not a claim about every salon. Confirm the actual date and replacement status when making a real booking.

C Notice the language

An apology needs useful information

A brief apology works best with a specific delay, its effect on the client, and a workable option. Do not pressure someone to accept a rushed service because they have already arrived. Explain the relevant local fee rule without presenting it as universal.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the delay
I am sorry; your appointment is running twenty minutes late.
State the revised start
The revised start is 2:20.
Ask about the limit
What time do you need to leave?
State the ordinary duration
We normally allow forty minutes for this service.
Explain the arithmetic
A 2:20 start would ordinarily finish at 3:00.
Name the conflict
That would be after your 2:45 departure deadline.
Avoid a rushed promise
I cannot promise a shortened service to make it fit.
Offer a concrete alternative
We have 10:00 tomorrow available.
Explain the local policy
We waive the change fee when our delay causes this rescheduling.
Invite the choice
Would you like the appointment moved to that slot?
Confirm acceptance
You would like tomorrow at 10:00 instead.
Replace the original
I will make this the replacement for today's appointment.
Avoid a duplicate
Today's slot will not remain as a second active booking.
Read back details
Let us confirm the date, time, and service together.
Document the reason
I will record the salon-caused delay.
Close with a check
Please check that the confirmation shows the agreed replacement.
6 language and precision notes
Running late
This describes schedule delay without necessarily blaming the client.
At versus by
At 2:20 gives a start; by 2:45 gives a latest departure.
Duration versus delay
Forty minutes is service time; twenty minutes is the delay.
Instead
Tomorrow's slot replaces today's rather than adding a second service.
Waive versus refund
Waiving a fee means not charging it; refunding returns money already paid.
Relative dates
Tomorrow is clear in conversation but a real confirmation should identify the actual date.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which explanation is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. Starting at 2:20 would ordinarily finish at 3:00, after your departure deadline.

The calculation preserves the ordinary duration and shows the fifteen-minute conflict.

2. Why is the change fee waived here?

Answer and explanation

C. The salon's delay causes the rescheduling under its stated policy.

The brief gives a specific local policy and a salon-caused delay.

3. Which booking record is correct after acceptance?

Answer and explanation

D. Tomorrow 10:00 replaces today's appointment.

The accepted option is a replacement, not a second active appointment.

4. Which response should be avoided?

Answer and explanation

C. We will definitely compress the forty-minute service into twenty-five minutes.

No shortened service has been promised or established as feasible.

E Conversations

The appointment no longer fits

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Luis: Client / Hana: Receptionist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • duplicate booking
  • booking record
  • service duration
  • replacement booking
  • revised start
  • availability
  • confirmation message
  • departure deadline
  • delay
  • fee waiver
  1. Luis
    I am here for my two o'clock haircut. Before I sit down, I should mention that I have to leave by quarter to three.
  2. Hana
    I am sorry, but we have a twenty-minute 1 today. I want to explain the timing clearly so you can decide whether another appointment would work better.
  3. Luis
    Thank you for telling me. Does that mean I would start at twenty past two, or is that when the haircut would be finished?
  4. Hana
    Twenty past two is the 2. We normally allow forty minutes for this service, so it would not be finished at that point.
  5. Luis
    Then I think we have a problem. I cannot move the time I need to leave, and I do not want to be watching the clock throughout.
  6. Hana
    I understand. With the usual 3, the ordinary finish would be three o'clock. That is fifteen minutes after you need to leave.
  7. Luis
    Could you squeeze it into twenty-five minutes? I really do have to be out by quarter to three.
  8. Hana
    I cannot promise a shortened service to meet your 4. The straightforward alternative is to move the appointment to a time that allows the normal service allocation.
  9. Luis
    What do you have tomorrow? If there is a morning appointment, that would probably be easier than trying to make today work.
  10. Hana
    There is 5 at ten tomorrow morning. We can discuss moving your haircut to that slot rather than keeping you waiting for a service that conflicts with your schedule.
  11. Luis
    Ten works for me. Would I have to pay a change fee even though the appointment is moving because the salon is behind?
  12. Hana
    Under our stated policy, we apply a 6 when our delay causes this rescheduling. You will not be charged that change fee for moving this appointment.
  13. Luis
    All right, please move it to ten tomorrow. I would prefer that to rushing today or being late for my next commitment.
  14. Hana
    I will confirm the 7 for tomorrow at ten. Today's appointment will no longer remain as an active second booking for the same service.
  15. Luis
    Thanks. Last time I changed an appointment, I got two reminders. Could you check that today is cancelled?
  16. Hana
    I will check the 8 when making the change. The record should show the agreed replacement and the salon-caused reason for rescheduling.
  17. Luis
    Could we read the details back before I go? I want to be certain I have heard the time correctly and that it is still the haircut.
  18. Hana
    Yes. The 9 should show the actual date for tomorrow, ten o'clock, and the haircut service. We will check those details together.
  19. Luis
    Yes, tomorrow at ten for the haircut. I'll check the date on the confirmation before I leave.
  20. Hana
    You are welcome, and I am sorry for the disruption. We have avoided a 10 and agreed on the new slot, with the change fee waived under our policy for this delay.

Answers and explanations
  1. delay Turn 2. Delay is the difference between the original 2:00 start and the revised 2:20 start, not the length of the haircut itself.
  2. revised start Turn 4. Revised start identifies 2:20 as the updated beginning, while the ordinary forty-minute service still has to follow.
  3. service duration Turn 6. Service duration is forty minutes; adding it to the revised 2:20 start produces a 3:00 ordinary finish.
  4. departure deadline Turn 8. Departure deadline is Luis's fixed latest leaving time of 2:45, which the ordinary revised schedule cannot meet.
  5. availability Turn 10. Availability means the 10:00 slot tomorrow can currently be offered; it becomes the replacement only after Luis chooses and confirms it.
  6. fee waiver Turn 12. Fee waiver means the salon does not charge the change fee in this salon-caused rescheduling; it is not a refund or universal rule.
  7. replacement booking Turn 14. Replacement booking means tomorrow's accepted appointment takes the place of today's slot rather than adding another active haircut booking.
  8. booking record Turn 16. Booking record holds the active appointment and change reason; updating it helps avoid conflicting reminders or duplicate active slots.
  9. confirmation message Turn 18. Confirmation message should state the actual date, 10:00 time, and haircut service so the client can verify the replacement details.
  10. duplicate booking Turn 20. Duplicate booking would leave two unintended active appointments; the accepted tomorrow slot replaces today's delayed appointment.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Luis: I am here for my two o'clock haircut. Before I sit down, I should mention that I have to leave by quarter to three.
  2. Hana: I am sorry, but we have a twenty-minute delay today. I want to explain the timing clearly so you can decide whether another appointment would work better.
  3. Luis: Thank you for telling me. Does that mean I would start at twenty past two, or is that when the haircut would be finished?
  4. Hana: Twenty past two is the revised start. We normally allow forty minutes for this service, so it would not be finished at that point.
  5. Luis: Then I think we have a problem. I cannot move the time I need to leave, and I do not want to be watching the clock throughout.
  6. Hana: I understand. With the usual service duration, the ordinary finish would be three o'clock. That is fifteen minutes after you need to leave.
  7. Luis: Could you squeeze it into twenty-five minutes? I really do have to be out by quarter to three.
  8. Hana: I cannot promise a shortened service to meet your departure deadline. The straightforward alternative is to move the appointment to a time that allows the normal service allocation.
  9. Luis: What do you have tomorrow? If there is a morning appointment, that would probably be easier than trying to make today work.
  10. Hana: There is availability at ten tomorrow morning. We can discuss moving your haircut to that slot rather than keeping you waiting for a service that conflicts with your schedule.
  11. Luis: Ten works for me. Would I have to pay a change fee even though the appointment is moving because the salon is behind?
  12. Hana: Under our stated policy, we apply a fee waiver when our delay causes this rescheduling. You will not be charged that change fee for moving this appointment.
  13. Luis: All right, please move it to ten tomorrow. I would prefer that to rushing today or being late for my next commitment.
  14. Hana: I will confirm the replacement booking for tomorrow at ten. Today's appointment will no longer remain as an active second booking for the same service.
  15. Luis: Thanks. Last time I changed an appointment, I got two reminders. Could you check that today is cancelled?
  16. Hana: I will check the booking record when making the change. The record should show the agreed replacement and the salon-caused reason for rescheduling.
  17. Luis: Could we read the details back before I go? I want to be certain I have heard the time correctly and that it is still the haircut.
  18. Hana: Yes. The confirmation message should show the actual date for tomorrow, ten o'clock, and the haircut service. We will check those details together.
  19. Luis: Yes, tomorrow at ten for the haircut. I'll check the date on the confirmation before I leave.
  20. Hana: You are welcome, and I am sorry for the disruption. We have avoided a duplicate booking and agreed on the new slot, with the change fee waived under our policy for this delay.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The departure time cannot move

Reception discusses the delayed 2:00 haircut.

  1. Receptionist: I'm sorry, we're running twenty minutes behind your two o'clock appointment this afternoon.
  2. Client: I need to leave by quarter to three. Is the full haircut still possible?
  3. Receptionist: The usual forty-minute service from 2:20 would finish around three, beyond your departure.
  4. Client: Could the stylist just work faster? I already arranged transport for today.
  5. Receptionist: I can't promise a compressed service. There is a full slot tomorrow at ten.
  6. Client: Tomorrow is possible, but would changing it count as my cancelling at short notice?
  7. Receptionist: This change is due to our delay; I can move it without a change fee.
  8. Client: Then please move it. I don't want to feel rushed through the consultation.
  9. Receptionist: You're booked tomorrow at ten for the full slot, and today's appointment is replaced.
  10. Client: Thanks for stating the timing clearly. I'll rearrange my ride for the new appointment.
A walk-in between booked clients

A barber explains current availability to a visitor.

  1. Barber: Are you looking for a haircut today, or checking our next available booking first?
  2. Client: I was hoping to walk in. I can see an empty chair by the window.
  3. Barber: That chair has a booking starting shortly; an empty chair doesn't always mean available time.
  4. Client: I only need a small change. Could I take part of that appointment slot?
  5. Barber: We need enough time for the complete visit. The next open haircut slot is four.
  6. Client: I could return at four. Would I still have to join the walk-in queue then?
  7. Barber: If we book that slot now, you'll have an appointment rather than a queue place.
  8. Client: Let's do that. Can you confirm which barber I'm seeing when I return?
  9. Barber: You'll see Jordan at four. Please allow the full booked period for your visit.
  10. Client: Great. I'll come back for the appointment instead of waiting beside the empty chair.
An offer is not a booking

A salon receptionist calls a client on the waiting list.

  1. Receptionist: A cancellation has opened tomorrow at eleven. Are you still interested in that time?
  2. Client: Possibly, but I need to check whether my colleague can cover my shift first.
  3. Receptionist: I can hold this offered slot until four today while you check with them.
  4. Client: Does that mean it is already booked, even if I don't call you back?
  5. Receptionist: No. It is temporarily held; we need your confirmation before four to book it.
  6. Client: Understood. My colleague will be back at three, so I can answer after that.
  7. Receptionist: Would a call to this number be the easiest way to confirm your decision?
  8. Client: Yes. If I can't take it, I'd still like to stay on the waiting list.
  9. Receptionist: That's fine. Today's hold expires at four, but your waiting-list request can remain.
  10. Client: I'll call with a definite answer after three rather than assuming the hold is permanent.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The appointment no longer fits
  1. Read the timing exchange. Say 2:20, forty minutes, 3:00, and 2:45 distinctly.
  2. Switch roles and read the replacement booking. Stress the waived change fee without implying that the haircut is free.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Calculate a different scheduling conflict

A 1:00 appointment is delayed to 1:15. Its usual duration is forty-five minutes. The client must leave at 1:50 and accepts a 9:30 replacement tomorrow. The salon waives the change fee for its own delay.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Reception: "The ordinary finish would be ___."

Answer and explanation

B. 2:00

Forty-five minutes after 1:15 gives an ordinary finish of 2:00.

2. Client: "That is ___ minutes after my departure deadline."

Answer and explanation

B. ten

The ordinary 2:00 finish is ten minutes later than 1:50.

3. Reception: "Tomorrow's replacement is at ___."

Answer and explanation

B. 9:30

The client accepts the specific 9:30 alternative supplied in the brief.

4. Reception: "The change fee is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. waived

The stated policy removes this fee because the salon caused the delay.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 04: Booking time and handling appointment changes
Original fictional case: Receptionist Hana tells client Luis that the 2:00 haircut appointment is running twenty minutes late. The usual service duration is forty minutes, while Luis must leave by 2:45. A 2:20 start would ordinarily finish at 3:00, so the appointment no longer fits the departure requirement. A 10:00 appointment tomorrow is available. This fictional salon waives the change fee when its own delay causes this rescheduling. Luis chooses tomorrow at 10:00, and Hana confirms the replacement appointment without keeping today as an active second booking.
Language workshop: The appointment no longer fits
Communication goal: Explain a delay, calculate the earliest ordinary finish, and offer a reschedule without promising a shortened service.
Language guidance: The delay changes the start, not the service duration. A 2:20 start plus forty minutes gives 3:00, fifteen minutes after the client must leave. Do not imply the ordinary service can simply fit into twenty-five minutes.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I am sorry; your appointment is running twenty minutes late. / The revised start is 2:20. / What time do you need to leave? / We normally allow forty minutes for this service. / A 2:20 start would ordinarily finish at 3:00. / That would be after your 2:45 departure deadline. / I cannot promise a shortened service to make it fit. / We have 10:00 tomorrow available. / We waive the change fee when our delay causes this rescheduling. / Would you like the appointment moved to that slot? / You would like tomorrow at 10:00 instead. / I will make this the replacement for today's appointment. / Today's slot will not remain as a second active booking. / Let us confirm the date, time, and service together. / I will record the salon-caused delay. / Please check that the confirmation shows the agreed replacement.
Vocabulary: appointment slot: Specific time reserved for a service. / scheduled start: Originally planned beginning time. / delay: Difference between the planned and later expected start. / revised start: Updated beginning time following a change. / service duration: Amount of time normally allocated to the service. / estimated finish: Expected ending time based on available information. / departure deadline: Latest time the client must leave. / time conflict: Incompatibility between two timing requirements. / availability: Whether a slot can currently be offered. / reschedule: Move an appointment to a different time. / replacement booking: New appointment taking the place of the original. / cancellation: Removal of an appointment from the schedule. / change fee: Charge associated with altering a booking under a stated policy. / fee waiver: Decision not to charge an otherwise applicable fee. / salon-caused delay: Delay originating from the salon's schedule or operations. / late arrival: Client reaching the appointment after its scheduled start. / no-show: Failure to attend without the required notice under a local policy. / notice period: Required advance time for a change under a specified policy. / confirmation message: Written or spoken statement of agreed booking details. / booking record: Stored appointment details and status. / duplicate booking: Two appointments unintentionally active for the same intended service. / waitlist: List of clients seeking a slot if one becomes available. / service allocation: Time assigned to carry out the planned service. / calendar date: Specific day identified by date, not only a relative term.
Speaking task: Read the timing exchange. Say 2:20, forty minutes, 3:00, and 2:45 distinctly. Switch roles and read the replacement booking. Stress the waived change fee without implying that the haircut is free. Complete the transfer, check the calculation in the key, and read the replacement time as a replacement, not an additional booking.
Partner: Luis: Client / Hana: Receptionist
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A 1:00 appointment is delayed to 1:15. Its usual duration is forty-five minutes. The client must leave at 1:50 and accepts a 9:30 replacement tomorrow. The salon waives the change fee for its own delay.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Luis: Could we read the details back before I go? I want to be certain I have heard the time correctly and that it is still the haircut.
Hana: Yes. The confirmation message should show the actual date for tomorrow, ten o'clock, and the haircut service. We will check those details together.
Luis: Yes, tomorrow at ten for the haircut. I'll check the date on the confirmation before I leave.
Hana: You are welcome, and I am sorry for the disruption. We have avoided a duplicate booking and agreed on the new slot, with the change fee waived under our policy for this delay.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Hairdressing and Barbering English
Professional audience: Hairstylists, barbers, salon assistants, and salon reception staff
Scope: All salons, clients, prices, photographs described in words, and incidents are fictional. Lesson 04: Booking time and handling appointment changes
Original fictional case: Receptionist Hana tells client Luis that the 2:00 haircut appointment is running twenty minutes late. The usual service duration is forty minutes, while Luis must leave by 2:45. A 2:20 start would ordinarily finish at 3:00, so the appointment no longer fits the departure requirement. A 10:00 appointment tomorrow is available. This fictional salon waives the change fee when its own delay causes this rescheduling. Luis chooses tomorrow at 10:00, and Hana confirms the replacement appointment without keeping today as an active second booking.
Language workshop: The appointment no longer fits
Communication goal: Explain a delay, calculate the earliest ordinary finish, and offer a reschedule without promising a shortened service.
Language guidance: The delay changes the start, not the service duration. A 2:20 start plus forty minutes gives 3:00, fifteen minutes after the client must leave. Do not imply the ordinary service can simply fit into twenty-five minutes.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I am sorry; your appointment is running twenty minutes late. / The revised start is 2:20. / What time do you need to leave? / We normally allow forty minutes for this service. / A 2:20 start would ordinarily finish at 3:00. / That would be after your 2:45 departure deadline. / I cannot promise a shortened service to make it fit. / We have 10:00 tomorrow available. / We waive the change fee when our delay causes this rescheduling. / Would you like the appointment moved to that slot? / You would like tomorrow at 10:00 instead. / I will make this the replacement for today's appointment. / Today's slot will not remain as a second active booking. / Let us confirm the date, time, and service together. / I will record the salon-caused delay. / Please check that the confirmation shows the agreed replacement.
Vocabulary: appointment slot: Specific time reserved for a service. / scheduled start: Originally planned beginning time. / delay: Difference between the planned and later expected start. / revised start: Updated beginning time following a change. / service duration: Amount of time normally allocated to the service. / estimated finish: Expected ending time based on available information. / departure deadline: Latest time the client must leave. / time conflict: Incompatibility between two timing requirements. / availability: Whether a slot can currently be offered. / reschedule: Move an appointment to a different time. / replacement booking: New appointment taking the place of the original. / cancellation: Removal of an appointment from the schedule. / change fee: Charge associated with altering a booking under a stated policy. / fee waiver: Decision not to charge an otherwise applicable fee. / salon-caused delay: Delay originating from the salon's schedule or operations. / late arrival: Client reaching the appointment after its scheduled start. / no-show: Failure to attend without the required notice under a local policy. / notice period: Required advance time for a change under a specified policy. / confirmation message: Written or spoken statement of agreed booking details. / booking record: Stored appointment details and status. / duplicate booking: Two appointments unintentionally active for the same intended service. / waitlist: List of clients seeking a slot if one becomes available. / service allocation: Time assigned to carry out the planned service. / calendar date: Specific day identified by date, not only a relative term.
Speaking task: Read the timing exchange. Say 2:20, forty minutes, 3:00, and 2:45 distinctly. Switch roles and read the replacement booking. Stress the waived change fee without implying that the haircut is free. Complete the transfer, check the calculation in the key, and read the replacement time as a replacement, not an additional booking.
Partner: Luis: Client / Hana: Receptionist
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A 1:00 appointment is delayed to 1:15. Its usual duration is forty-five minutes. The client must leave at 1:50 and accepts a 9:30 replacement tomorrow. The salon waives the change fee for its own delay.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Luis: Could we read the details back before I go? I want to be certain I have heard the time correctly and that it is still the haircut.
Hana: Yes. The confirmation message should show the actual date for tomorrow, ten o'clock, and the haircut service. We will check those details together.
Luis: Yes, tomorrow at ten for the haircut. I'll check the date on the confirmation before I leave.
Hana: You are welcome, and I am sorry for the disruption. We have avoided a duplicate booking and agreed on the new slot, with the change fee waived under our policy for this delay.
END REFERENCE
05Checking comfort and pausing clearlyPause before the wash+

Your goal

Acknowledge discomfort, clarify the client description, and arrange a check without diagnosing or prescribing a position.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

Pause before the wash

Sam reported neck pressure before water started. Imani immediately stopped preparation and attended to the concern through the salon process. The dialogue takes place afterward: Sam is away from the basin and reports feeling comfortable. Noor, the lead stylist, has been asked to review the concern. Sam agrees to review only, not further preparation or washing. No cause or injury has been established. This is not a positioning or treatment demonstration; immediate assistance must not wait for a twenty-turn conversation, and medical or emergency help follows actual response procedures when needed.

Sam: Client / Imani: Salon assistant

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What has not started?

Answer and explanation

B. The water and wash

The brief places the concern before water begins and before the wash starts.

2. Where does Sam describe pressure?

Answer and explanation

C. At the back of the neck against the support

Sam identifies the contact area at the back of the neck.

3. What does Sam agree to?

Answer and explanation

D. A comfort check, not starting the wash

Consent is limited to the check; preparation remains paused.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Sam reports pressure where the neck meets the support. That description is not a diagnosis. The water has not started, preparation is paused, and Noor is asked to review the concern before any decision to continue.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
wash station
Area equipped for washing a client's hair.identify the wash station
basin
Bowl at the wash station.refer to the basin
neck support
Surface or fitting supporting the neck at the basin.check the neck support
contact point
Place where the body touches a support or surface.identify the contact point
pressure
Sensation of force against an area, described by the client.describe the pressure
discomfort
Unpleasant sensation that the client reports.acknowledge discomfort
comfort check
Review of the client's comfort before proceeding.arrange a comfort check
preparation
Work done before the wash itself starts.pause preparation
pause
Temporary stop pending clarification or a check.keep preparation paused
resume
Begin again after a pause with the required agreement.confirm before resuming
consent to continue
Agreement to proceed with a specific next action.ask for consent to continue
client report
The client's own account of a sensation or concern.preserve the client report
location
Specific area where the sensation is felt.clarify the location
onset
When a sensation began, without diagnosing its cause.ask about onset
intensity
How strong a sensation feels to the client.clarify reported intensity
persistent
Continuing rather than stopping immediately.describe persistent discomfort
intermittent
Occurring at intervals rather than continuously.describe intermittent pressure
sensitivity
Reported discomfort or response to contact, without a diagnosis.record reported sensitivity
scalp
Skin covering the head beneath the hair.distinguish scalp from neck
support adjustment
Change to a support, subject to appropriate assessment.request a support adjustment review
positioning
How the client is placed, not a prescription made by this exercise.refer a positioning concern
lead stylist
Senior salon colleague handling the relevant service concern.contact the lead stylist
reassurance
Supportive communication that should not become an unsupported safety guarantee.offer measured reassurance
agreed next step
Specific action the client and staff have confirmed.state the agreed next step

Keep permission specific: yes to a comfort check does not mean yes to the wash. Do not prescribe a new position, dismiss the sensation as normal, or promise that changing the support will resolve an unknown cause.

C Notice the language

A pause is a service response

Some clients minimize discomfort because they do not want to interrupt the appointment. Respond plainly and respectfully when they speak up. Agreement to a comfort check is not the same as agreement to restart; ask about the specific next step.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge promptly
Thank you for telling me; we will pause here.
Confirm the service state
The water has not started.
Ask for location
Where are you feeling the pressure?
Use the client's description
You feel pressure where your neck meets the support.
Avoid a diagnosis
I cannot determine the cause from that description.
Keep the pause explicit
Preparation remains paused while we check.
Ask permission for review
Would you like me to ask Noor to review the comfort concern?
Name the next person
Noor is the lead stylist handling this check.
Separate consent
Agreeing to the check does not mean starting the wash.
Avoid minimization
I will not assume the discomfort is something you should tolerate.
Preserve the report
I will pass on your description in your own terms.
Do not delay assistance
We will attend to your discomfort immediately; the longer discussion can wait.
Check understanding
Is the pressure at that contact point what you want me to report?
Avoid a guarantee
We have not established what will resolve it.
Confirm the boundary
We have not agreed to resume yet.
Close the immediate exchange
The next step is the comfort check, with the preparation still paused.
6 language and precision notes
Feels versus is
Feels uncomfortable reports experience; is injured asserts a condition not established here.
Pause versus cancel
A pause stops the current action without automatically ending every future service.
Before
Before water starts fixes the sequence and prevents an inaccurate incident account.
Permission scope
Permission for review applies to review, not every later action.
Client wording
Specific reported pressure is more accurate than an invented medical label.
No minimization
Polite reassurance should not pressure a client to continue through discomfort.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which report is factual?

Answer and explanation

A. Sam describes pressure at the back of the neck against the support.

The correct statement preserves the client's reported sensation without assigning cause or diagnosis.

2. Which action is agreed?

Answer and explanation

A. Ask Noor for a comfort check while preparation stays paused.

The client consents to review only, with no agreement to start washing.

3. Which reassurance overreaches?

Answer and explanation

B. This is definitely harmless, so we can carry on.

Harmlessness is not established, and the client has not agreed to continue.

4. Which chronology is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. Discomfort was reported before water started.

The supplied sequence places the concern before water or washing begins.

Answers and explanations
  1. preparation Turn 2. Preparation stopped before any water or washing began, so the sequence must stay accurate.
  2. discomfort Turn 4. Discomfort is the sensation Sam reported, not proof of a particular injury or cause.
  3. lead stylist Turn 6. Noor is the colleague arranging this salon review, not a substitute for medical or emergency care.
  4. contact point Turn 8. The contact point locates the pressure where the neck met the support.
  5. client report Turn 10. The client report preserves Sam's own description without adding an unsupported explanation.
  6. water Turn 12. Water never started, so temperature cannot be invented as an observed cause.
  7. comfort check Turn 14. The comfort check is a review, not permission to carry out the wash.
  8. consent Turn 16. Consent concerns a specific next action; improvement alone is not permission to proceed.
  9. resume Turn 18. Resume refers to paused preparation; the wash itself has not begun at all.
  10. agreed next step Turn 20. Sam accepts the review only, while preparation remains paused and washing has not begun.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Sam: The pressure was right at the back of my neck. Thank you for stopping; I feel comfortable now that we're away from the basin.
  2. Imani: I'm glad you told me. The preparation is still paused. We won't begin washing while this is unresolved.
  3. Sam: I thought I should mention it straight away, but I was worried about holding up the appointment.
  4. Imani: Please tell us. You don't need to tolerate discomfort to keep the appointment moving. Noor can review the concern with you.
  5. Sam: Is Noor the person who checks the wash station? I don't know who I should explain it to.
  6. Imani: Noor is our lead stylist. I've asked for the review. We follow our response procedures if further help is needed.
  7. Sam: All right. It was where my neck touched the support, not across my scalp. Please make that clear.
  8. Imani: I'll describe that contact point exactly. Was that the wording you wanted me to pass on?
  9. Sam: Yes. I can't tell whether it was the support or the position. I only know how it felt.
  10. Imani: Your client report is enough to explain the concern. I won't guess the cause.
  11. Sam: Please don't write that the water was too hot. You hadn't turned it on.
  12. Imani: Correct. The water hadn't started. The report will say pressure during preparation, before the wash.
  13. Sam: Can Noor look at it without us starting again? I'd like to hear what's proposed first.
  14. Imani: Yes. The comfort check is separate from agreeing to continue. Asking for it doesn't commit you to another attempt.
  15. Sam: Good. I feel better now, but I don't want that taken as an automatic yes.
  16. Imani: Understood. Feeling comfortable now doesn't give consent for the next step. We'll ask you directly.
  17. Sam: If we do continue, will you explain anything that needs to change before you do it?
  18. Imani: Yes. Before we resume preparation, the proposal needs to be clear and agreed. I can't promise an unassessed adjustment will solve it.
  19. Sam: Then please keep it paused. I'm agreeing to speak with Noor, not to have my hair washed yet.
  20. Imani: That's the agreed next step: review with Noor, your report passed on, and no washing started.
Dialogue in the book, page 46 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Pressure before the water starts

A stylist checks a client's comfort at the basin.

  1. Stylist: Before we start the water, how does the neck support feel in this position?
  2. Client: It presses at the back. I was hoping I could just ignore it briefly.
  3. Stylist: You don't need to ignore discomfort. I'll pause here while we discuss the arrangement.
  4. Client: Could I sit up first? It's easier to show you where the pressure is.
  5. Stylist: Of course. Tell me whether you want to leave the basin while we talk.
  6. Client: Yes, please. I'm sorry if that means the appointment takes longer than expected.
  7. Stylist: Thank you for telling me. We need your comfort feedback, not an apology.
  8. Client: Can the other chair be considered, or is this the only arrangement available today?
  9. Stylist: I'll ask my colleague to review the available setup with you before washing starts.
  10. Client: That's what I need. Let's keep the wash paused until we've discussed it.
A cape that catches on a collar

A barber responds to a client's comfort request.

  1. Barber: Is there anything about the cape or chair you want me to check first?
  2. Client: The cape feels tight over my collar. Could we pause for a moment?
  3. Barber: Certainly. Is the pressure around the whole neck or mainly against the front?
  4. Client: Mainly the front, where the shirt button sits underneath the edge of the cape.
  5. Barber: Thank you. I'll stop preparing the cut so we can address that together.
  6. Client: I didn't want to interrupt after you'd started, so I'm mentioning it now.
  7. Barber: That's helpful. Please say something again if the arrangement still doesn't feel comfortable.
  8. Client: It already feels easier now that we've stopped. May I straighten my collar first?
  9. Barber: Yes, take your time. Then tell me how the cape feels before we continue.
  10. Client: My collar is settled now. Please check with me once the cape is back.
A fragrance preference is not a diagnosis

A client asks about a strongly scented styling product.

  1. Stylist: Before discussing the finish, is there a product preference you want me to know?
  2. Client: Strong fragrances bother me. I don't know whether that counts as an allergy.
  3. Stylist: I'll note that sensitivity to fragrance. Which product's scent is bothering you today?
  4. Client: The spray used nearby has a strong scent. I'd prefer not to use that.
  5. Stylist: We can leave that product out while we discuss your preferences and the available alternatives.
  6. Client: Could you tell me exactly which product you propose, instead of simply saying gentle?
  7. Stylist: Yes. We can check its label together; I won't promise it cannot cause a reaction.
  8. Client: I'd rather skip a finishing product today if we can't resolve my concern.
  9. Stylist: Understood. No finishing spray today, and your concern remains described in your own words.
  10. Client: Thank you. That choice is clear without putting a medical label on my preference.

Pause before the wash

  2. Switch roles. Stress before water started and review only when correcting the possible misunderstanding.

Answer and explanation

The concern occurs before water begins, preserving the correct chronology.

3. Assistant: "The agreed next step is a comfort ___."

Answer and explanation

Review is the action accepted by the client in this scenario.

4. Client: "I have not agreed to ___."

Answer and explanation

Agreement to review does not authorize restarting the paused wash.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 05: Checking comfort and pausing clearly
Original fictional case: Sam reported neck pressure before water started. Imani immediately stopped preparation and attended to the concern through the salon process. The dialogue takes place afterward: Sam is away from the basin and reports feeling comfortable. Noor, the lead stylist, has been asked to review the concern. Sam agrees to review only, not further preparation or washing. No cause or injury has been established. This is not a positioning or treatment demonstration; immediate assistance must not wait for a twenty-turn conversation, and medical or emergency help follows actual response procedures when needed.
Language workshop: Pause before the wash
Communication goal: Acknowledge discomfort, clarify the client description, and arrange a check without diagnosing or prescribing a position.
Language guidance: Sam reports pressure where the neck meets the support. That description is not a diagnosis. The water has not started, preparation is paused, and Noor is asked to review the concern before any decision to continue.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Thank you for telling me; we will pause here. / The water has not started. / Where are you feeling the pressure? / You feel pressure where your neck meets the support. / I cannot determine the cause from that description. / Preparation remains paused while we check. / Would you like me to ask Noor to review the comfort concern? / Noor is the lead stylist handling this check. / Agreeing to the check does not mean starting the wash. / I will not assume the discomfort is something you should tolerate. / I will pass on your description in your own terms. / We will attend to your discomfort immediately; the longer discussion can wait. / Is the pressure at that contact point what you want me to report? / We have not established what will resolve it. / We have not agreed to resume yet. / The next step is the comfort check, with the preparation still paused.
Vocabulary: wash station: Area equipped for washing a client's hair. / basin: Bowl at the wash station. / neck support: Surface or fitting supporting the neck at the basin. / contact point: Place where the body touches a support or surface. / pressure: Sensation of force against an area, described by the client. / discomfort: Unpleasant sensation that the client reports. / comfort check: Review of the client's comfort before proceeding. / preparation: Work done before the wash itself starts. / pause: Temporary stop pending clarification or a check. / resume: Begin again after a pause with the required agreement. / consent to continue: Agreement to proceed with a specific next action. / client report: The client's own account of a sensation or concern. / location: Specific area where the sensation is felt. / onset: When a sensation began, without diagnosing its cause. / intensity: How strong a sensation feels to the client. / persistent: Continuing rather than stopping immediately. / intermittent: Occurring at intervals rather than continuously. / sensitivity: Reported discomfort or response to contact, without a diagnosis. / scalp: Skin covering the head beneath the hair. / support adjustment: Change to a support, subject to appropriate assessment. / positioning: How the client is placed, not a prescription made by this exercise. / lead stylist: Senior salon colleague handling the relevant service concern. / reassurance: Supportive communication that should not become an unsupported safety guarantee. / agreed next step: Specific action the client and staff have confirmed.
Speaking task: Read the exchange as a follow-up after immediate assistance, not while leaving a client uncomfortable. Switch roles. Stress before water started and review only when correcting the possible misunderstanding. Complete and check the comfort transfer. Read the limited agreement without treating it as permission to wash.
Partner: Sam: Client / Imani: Salon assistant
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Before water starts, a client reports pressure behind the neck against the support. Preparation is paused. The client agrees to a comfort review with lead stylist Jo but has not agreed to resume.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Sam: If we do continue, will you explain anything that needs to change before you do it?
Imani: Yes. Before we resume preparation, the proposal needs to be clear and agreed. I can't promise an unassessed adjustment will solve it.
Sam: Then please keep it paused. I'm agreeing to speak with Noor, not to have my hair washed yet.
Imani: That's the agreed next step: review with Noor, your report passed on, and no washing started.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. 06Explaining services, prices, and product choicesWhat does forty-five include?+

Your goal

Explain an optional service, compare complete totals, and confirm a client choice before adding anything.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

What does forty-five include?

Client Aisha believes the advertised $45 haircut includes a finish. Stylist Ben explains that this fictional menu lists the haircut at $45 and an optional finish at $15, with no other charges in the exercise. Neither service has begun. The complete choices are $45 for the haircut only or $60 for both services. Aisha asks whether buying a styling product is required; Ben confirms it is not part of either quoted service choice. Aisha chooses the haircut only, with no finish or retail product added.

Aisha: Client / Ben: Stylist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the haircut-only total?

Answer and explanation

A. $45

The supplied menu sets the haircut alone at forty-five dollars.

2. What is the complete combined total?

Answer and explanation

B. $60

The forty-five-dollar haircut plus the fifteen-dollar optional finish totals sixty dollars.

3. What does Aisha choose?

Answer and explanation

D. Haircut only, with no finish or product added

The final choice explicitly excludes the optional finish and retail product.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The styling finish is optional and costs $15 in addition to the $45 haircut. Both services total $60; the haircut-only choice remains $45. There are no other charges in this fictional comparison, and no retail product is included.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
service menu
List describing available services and their prices.explain the service menu
base service
Main service before optional additions.identify the base service
optional finish
Separately offered final styling service in this menu.explain the optional finish
add-on
Additional item or service requiring agreement.confirm an add-on
inclusive price
Price covering the specified listed elements.clarify the inclusive price
combined total
Sum payable for the selected services together.state the combined total
itemized price
Price shown separately for each component.provide an itemized price
service inclusion
Element covered by a particular service description.clarify service inclusions
service exclusion
Element not covered by the quoted service.explain service exclusions
retail product
Product sold for the client to take away.discuss a retail product
product recommendation
Suggested product, not an automatic purchase.separate a recommendation from a sale
styling cream
Product category used to help shape or manage a style.describe a styling cream
mousse
Foam-format styling product.identify a mousse
gel
Gel-format product used for styling or hold.describe a gel
pomade
Styling-product category often associated with shape, control, or sheen.clarify a pomade preference
finishing spray
Spray product used as a finishing step, subject to client preference.discuss finishing spray
hold
Degree of styling control claimed for a product.compare hold descriptions
shine
Degree of visible sheen in a finish.describe the shine
matte finish
Low-shine appearance in a product or style description.clarify a matte finish
product claim
Statement made about a product's characteristics or effects.check a product claim
purchase decision
Client's choice whether to buy an item.confirm the purchase decision
service authorization
Permission to carry out the specified service.confirm service authorization
price misunderstanding
Difference between what the client expected and what the menu includes.resolve a price misunderstanding
charge confirmation
Read-back of the agreed amount and what it covers.give a charge confirmation

A recommendation is not a purchase decision. Product words such as hold, matte, and shine describe features to discuss; they do not establish suitability for every person or permission to add a product to the bill.

C Notice the language

Make the optional part genuinely optional

Clients may assume a familiar service name includes the same elements everywhere. Explain the specific menu without implying they should have known. State the full comparison before seeking a choice, and do not let a product recommendation become an unrequested purchase.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the expectation
I understand you thought the finish was included.
Refer to the specific menu
On this menu, the haircut is $45.
Identify the optional element
The finish is an optional $15 addition.
State the combined total
Both services together would be $60.
Preserve the base choice
The haircut-only option remains $45.
Clarify the comparison
Those are the complete totals for these two choices.
Ask before adding
Would you like the finish added, or the haircut only?
Separate recommendation and sale
A product recommendation does not add it to your purchase.
Confirm no purchase requirement
Neither service choice requires a retail-product purchase here.
Explain a product word
Matte describes a lower-shine appearance.
Avoid universal claims
We need to check the actual product information and your preference.
Confirm the decision
You have chosen the haircut only.
Record the exclusions
No finish or retail product will be added.
Resolve without blame
Let us make the inclusions clear before we begin.
Read back the charge
The agreed service is the haircut at $45.
Keep later changes explicit
We would confirm any later addition before proceeding.
6 language and precision notes
Includes versus costs extra
Includes places an element inside the price; costs extra marks a separate charge.
Optional
The client can decline without losing the stated base-service choice.
Together versus each
Together gives the combined total; each would imply separate equal amounts.
Would be
This expresses the price of an option before it is chosen.
Recommendation versus authorization
Suggesting a product does not authorize sale or application.
No other charges
This is a supplied fictional fact, not a rule for every real salon price.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which comparison is complete?

Answer and explanation

D. Haircut only $45; haircut plus finish $60.

The correct comparison gives both complete totals using the supplied menu.

2. Which question preserves a real choice?

Answer and explanation

C. Would you like the $60 combined option or the $45 haircut only?

The question presents both priced options before any service begins.

3. What does matte describe here?

Answer and explanation

D. A lower-shine appearance

Matte concerns appearance, not safety guarantees, purchase requirements, or haircut length.

4. Which final read-back matches Aisha's decision?

Answer and explanation

A. Haircut at $45, with no finish or retail product added.

The correct read-back preserves the selected base service and explicit exclusions.

E Conversations

What does forty-five include?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Aisha: Client / Ben: Stylist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • service menu
  • charge confirmation
  • purchase decision
  • combined total
  • service authorization
  • product recommendation
  • base service
  • optional finish
  • retail product
  • matte finish
  1. Aisha
    I thought the forty-five-dollar haircut included the finish. Before we start, can you explain what that price actually covers?
  2. Ben
    Of course. On this 1, the haircut is forty-five dollars, and the finish is listed separately as an optional fifteen-dollar addition.
  3. Aisha
    So if I have both, the final amount is not forty-five. I would like the complete number rather than just the extra amount.
  4. Ben
    The 2 for both services is sixty dollars. The haircut-only option is forty-five dollars, and those are the complete totals for these two choices.
  5. Aisha
    I hadn't realized it was separate. Can I have the haircut without paying for the extra styling?
  6. Ben
    The 3 is your choice. Neither service has begun, and I will confirm what you want before adding anything to the agreed plan.
  7. Aisha
    Can I just have the haircut today? I am keeping to a budget, and I would rather understand the basic option clearly.
  8. Ben
    Yes. The 4 here is the haircut at forty-five dollars. Declining the finish does not remove that option or turn it into the sixty-dollar combined service.
  9. Aisha
    I also heard someone mention a styling product. Do I have to buy one to have the haircut-only option at that price?
  10. Ben
    No. A 5 is not part of either quoted service choice, and neither requires a product purchase here. Discussing one does not mean adding it to your bill.
  11. Aisha
    Good. I thought buying something might be part of the deal. I'm not looking for a product today.
  12. Ben
    We can keep the 6 separate from the service decision. You can ask about a feature without agreeing to buy anything.
  13. Aisha
    For example, what does matte mean? I have seen it on products, but I am not sure whether it describes strength or something else.
  14. Ben
    A 7 describes a lower-shine appearance. Hold is a different feature, and the actual product information matters when comparing what a particular item claims.
  15. Aisha
    Ah, so matte doesn't tell me how strong the hold is. I was mixing those up.
  16. Ben
    Exactly. The 8 is separate from the discussion. I will not add a product simply because we have talked about its description.
  17. Aisha
    Then please keep today to the haircut only, at forty-five dollars. No optional finish and no retail product for me.
  18. Ben
    I will record that 9: haircut only at forty-five dollars, with no finish or retail product added. We have clarified the choice before either service begins.
  19. Aisha
    That's right. I'd rather know the price now than question it at the till.
  20. Ben
    Let me give the final 10: forty-five dollars for the haircut. Any later addition would need its own clear discussion and agreement; nothing extra has been selected today.

Answers and explanations
  1. service menu Turn 2. Service menu is the specific list governing this fictional price comparison; the client should not be expected to infer its inclusions from other salons.
  2. combined total Turn 4. Combined total adds the forty-five-dollar haircut and fifteen-dollar finish, giving sixty dollars with no other charges in the exercise.
  3. optional finish Turn 6. Optional finish is a separate service the client may accept or decline; it is not automatically authorized by choosing the haircut.
  4. base service Turn 8. Base service is the haircut alone at forty-five dollars, which remains available without the optional finish.
  5. retail product Turn 10. Retail product is a separate take-home purchase, not a required component of either quoted service choice in this scenario.
  6. product recommendation Turn 12. Product recommendation is a suggestion to discuss, not the client's authorization to buy or a condition of receiving the stated service.
  7. matte finish Turn 14. Matte finish describes a lower-shine appearance, not the degree of hold or a guarantee that a product suits every client.
  8. purchase decision Turn 16. Purchase decision belongs to the client and remains separate from asking questions or hearing a recommendation.
  9. service authorization Turn 18. Service authorization is limited to the haircut the client selects; it excludes the optional finish and any retail purchase.
  10. charge confirmation Turn 20. Charge confirmation repeats the agreed forty-five-dollar haircut and exclusions, resolving the original misunderstanding before work starts.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Aisha: I thought the forty-five-dollar haircut included the finish. Before we start, can you explain what that price actually covers?
  2. Ben: Of course. On this service menu, the haircut is forty-five dollars, and the finish is listed separately as an optional fifteen-dollar addition.
  3. Aisha: So if I have both, the final amount is not forty-five. I would like the complete number rather than just the extra amount.
  4. Ben: The combined total for both services is sixty dollars. The haircut-only option is forty-five dollars, and those are the complete totals for these two choices.
  5. Aisha: I hadn't realized it was separate. Can I have the haircut without paying for the extra styling?
  6. Ben: The optional finish is your choice. Neither service has begun, and I will confirm what you want before adding anything to the agreed plan.
  7. Aisha: Can I just have the haircut today? I am keeping to a budget, and I would rather understand the basic option clearly.
  8. Ben: Yes. The base service here is the haircut at forty-five dollars. Declining the finish does not remove that option or turn it into the sixty-dollar combined service.
  9. Aisha: I also heard someone mention a styling product. Do I have to buy one to have the haircut-only option at that price?
  10. Ben: No. A retail product is not part of either quoted service choice, and neither requires a product purchase here. Discussing one does not mean adding it to your bill.
  11. Aisha: Good. I thought buying something might be part of the deal. I'm not looking for a product today.
  12. Ben: We can keep the product recommendation separate from the service decision. You can ask about a feature without agreeing to buy anything.
  13. Aisha: For example, what does matte mean? I have seen it on products, but I am not sure whether it describes strength or something else.
  14. Ben: A matte finish describes a lower-shine appearance. Hold is a different feature, and the actual product information matters when comparing what a particular item claims.
  15. Aisha: Ah, so matte doesn't tell me how strong the hold is. I was mixing those up.
  16. Ben: Exactly. The purchase decision is separate from the discussion. I will not add a product simply because we have talked about its description.
  17. Aisha: Then please keep today to the haircut only, at forty-five dollars. No optional finish and no retail product for me.
  18. Ben: I will record that service authorization: haircut only at forty-five dollars, with no finish or retail product added. We have clarified the choice before either service begins.
  19. Aisha: That's right. I'd rather know the price now than question it at the till.
  20. Ben: Let me give the final charge confirmation: forty-five dollars for the haircut. Any later addition would need its own clear discussion and agreement; nothing extra has been selected today.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The finish is a separate choice

A stylist explains a fictional menu before starting: haircut forty-five dollars, optional finish fifteen, and no additional charges.

  1. Stylist: The service menu lists your haircut at forty-five. Did you want the fifteen-dollar finishing add-on?
  2. Client: I thought the forty-five-dollar price covered both. What would the complete amount be?
  3. Stylist: Both services would be sixty dollars altogether, with no other charges added to that amount.
  4. Client: I can spend sixty, but I'd like to know what choice I actually have.
  5. Stylist: You can select the haircut alone for forty-five or add the finish for fifteen.
  6. Client: Then I'll take the haircut alone. I don't need the extra finish this afternoon.
  7. Stylist: Certainly. I'll confirm the haircut-only booking and won't add the optional service later.
  8. Client: Could I have an itemized receipt showing just the service I chose today?
  9. Stylist: Yes, it will list the haircut at forty-five, with no separate finishing charge.
  10. Client: That's clear now. Thank you for explaining the difference before I made the decision.
A starting price needs a specific estimate

A client phones about a service menu.

  1. Receptionist: You asked about the color consultation. Which part of the menu price needs clarification?
  2. Client: It says starting at eighty. Does that mean every color appointment costs eighty dollars?
  3. Receptionist: No. That's the starting price, not a quote for every possible color service.
  4. Client: What would I need before deciding whether a particular appointment fits my budget?
  5. Receptionist: The stylist needs to assess the requested service and explain a specific estimate.
  6. Client: Could I attend the consultation without committing to buy the service immediately afterward?
  7. Receptionist: Yes. Today's offer is a consultation, and its stated fee is separate from treatment.
  8. Client: Please tell me that fee too, so I can compare what I'm actually booking.
  9. Receptionist: The consultation is twenty dollars here. Any later service estimate would be discussed separately.
  10. Client: Then book the consultation only. I'll decide about a service after hearing its estimate.
A product quantity is not a discount

A client compares two sizes of styling cream.

  1. Stylist: These are the two sizes you asked about: fifty milliliters and one hundred milliliters.
  2. Client: The larger one costs more overall. Is it still cheaper for the same amount?
  3. Stylist: The small one is twelve dollars; the large one is twenty dollars for twice the quantity.
  4. Client: So two small containers would cost twenty-four, compared with twenty for the large one?
  5. Stylist: That's right for these displayed prices. Which size fits your likely use and budget?
  6. Client: The small one. I haven't tried it, and I don't want a bigger purchase today.
  7. Stylist: Certainly. Choosing the smaller size is fine even though its price per milliliter is higher.
  8. Client: Will buying it affect the haircut price or add anything else to my booking?
  9. Stylist: No. It's a separate twelve-dollar product purchase; your agreed haircut charge doesn't change.
  10. Client: Then I'll take one small container. Please list it separately on the receipt.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

What does forty-five include?
  1. Read both complete prices: $45 for the haircut and $60 with the optional styling finish.
  2. Switch roles and read the product exchange. Contrast matte appearance with hold; retain the no-purchase decision.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Answer and explanation

The selected base service retains its fifty-dollar price without the finish.

3. Stylist: "The finish is ___."

Answer and explanation

The client can decline the separately priced finish in this menu.

4. Client: "No retail product is ___."

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.
