Lesson 06: Explaining services, prices, and product choices
Original fictional case: Client Aisha believes the advertised $45 haircut includes a finish. Stylist Ben explains that this fictional menu lists the haircut at $45 and an optional finish at $15, with no other charges in the exercise. Neither service has begun. The complete choices are $45 for the haircut only or $60 for both services. Aisha asks whether buying a styling product is required; Ben confirms it is not part of either quoted service choice. Aisha chooses the haircut only, with no finish or retail product added.
Language workshop: What does forty-five include?
Communication goal: Explain an optional service, compare complete totals, and confirm a client choice before adding anything.
Language guidance: The styling finish is optional and costs $15 in addition to the $45 haircut. Both services total $60; the haircut-only choice remains $45. There are no other charges in this fictional comparison, and no retail product is included.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand you thought the finish was included. / On this menu, the haircut is $45. / The finish is an optional $15 addition. / Both services together would be $60. / The haircut-only option remains $45. / Those are the complete totals for these two choices. / Would you like the finish added, or the haircut only? / A product recommendation does not add it to your purchase. / Neither service choice requires a retail-product purchase here. / Matte describes a lower-shine appearance. / We need to check the actual product information and your preference. / You have chosen the haircut only. / No finish or retail product will be added. / Let us make the inclusions clear before we begin. / The agreed service is the haircut at $45. / We would confirm any later addition before proceeding.
Vocabulary: service menu: List describing available services and their prices. / base service: Main service before optional additions. / optional finish: Separately offered final styling service in this menu. / add-on: Additional item or service requiring agreement. / inclusive price: Price covering the specified listed elements. / combined total: Sum payable for the selected services together. / itemized price: Price shown separately for each component. / service inclusion: Element covered by a particular service description. / service exclusion: Element not covered by the quoted service. / retail product: Product sold for the client to take away. / product recommendation: Suggested product, not an automatic purchase. / styling cream: Product category used to help shape or manage a style. / mousse: Foam-format styling product. / gel: Gel-format product used for styling or hold. / pomade: Styling-product category often associated with shape, control, or sheen. / finishing spray: Spray product used as a finishing step, subject to client preference. / hold: Degree of styling control claimed for a product. / shine: Degree of visible sheen in a finish. / matte finish: Low-shine appearance in a product or style description. / product claim: Statement made about a product's characteristics or effects. / purchase decision: Client's choice whether to buy an item. / service authorization: Permission to carry out the specified service. / price misunderstanding: Difference between what the client expected and what the menu includes. / charge confirmation: Read-back of the agreed amount and what it covers.
Speaking task: Read both complete prices: $45 for the haircut and $60 with the optional styling finish. Switch roles and read the product exchange. Contrast matte appearance with hold; retain the no-purchase decision. Complete and check the price transfer. Read the selected service and excluded extras before the final total.
Partner: Aisha: Client / Ben: Stylist
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A fictional menu lists a haircut at $50 and an optional finish at $20, with no other charges. The client chooses the haircut only and declines a retail product. Nothing has begun.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Aisha: Then please keep today to the haircut only, at forty-five dollars. No optional finish and no retail product for me.
Ben: I will record that service authorization: haircut only at forty-five dollars, with no finish or retail product added. We have clarified the choice before either service begins.
Aisha: That's right. I'd rather know the price now than question it at the till.
Ben: Let me give the final charge confirmation: forty-five dollars for the haircut. Any later addition would need its own clear discussion and agreement; nothing extra has been selected today.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Responding to a concern about the resultThe left side looks longer+

Your goal

Acknowledge a specific result concern, separate appearance from confirmed findings, and arrange review before promising a remedy.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

The left side looks longer

At checkout, client Mei says the left side of the haircut looks longer. Receptionist Rafael hears that the earlier consultation promised an even outline. The current appearance has not been reviewed together with stylist Jo. Rafael pauses checkout and offers a mirror review with Jo. Mei accepts the review. No cause has been established, and neither a correction nor a refund has been approved. The conversation must not turn a reported concern into a confirmed cutting error, a promise to remove more hair, or an automatic payment resolution.

Mei: Client / Rafael: Receptionist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does Mei report?

Answer and explanation

A. The left side looks longer

The concern is a reported visual difference that has not yet been reviewed.

2. What was the earlier agreed expectation?

Answer and explanation

B. An even outline

The consultation reportedly promised an even outline, making that expectation relevant to review.

3. What is accepted now?

Answer and explanation

B. A mirror review with Jo

Mei accepts the review while correction and payment decisions remain unapproved.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Mei reports that the left side looks longer; no joint review has confirmed a cause or cutting error. The earlier expectation was an even outline. Acknowledging both facts does not authorize removing more hair.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
result concern
Client's question or dissatisfaction about the service outcome.acknowledge a result concern
even outline
Outer edge intended to look balanced in length.review the even outline
apparent difference
Difference that seems visible before assessment confirms details.describe the apparent difference
left side
Client's left-hand side, clarified from the client's perspective.identify the left side
right side
Client's right-hand side, distinguished from a mirror image.compare the right side
mirror review
Joint examination of the appearance using a mirror.arrange a mirror review
hand mirror
Portable mirror used to show another view.offer a hand mirror
viewing angle
Direction from which the result is being seen.clarify the viewing angle
client perspective
The client's view or orientation, important for side references.confirm the client perspective
reported expectation
What the client says was agreed earlier.record the reported expectation
consultation note
Record of preferences and agreed service details.check the consultation note
assessment finding
Conclusion reached through the relevant review.distinguish an assessment finding
cause
Reason for the apparent difference, not yet established.avoid inventing a cause
correction
Additional work intended to address a confirmed concern.discuss a proposed correction
remedy
Proposed response to a service concern.clarify the proposed remedy
rework consent
Permission for specifically described additional service work.confirm rework consent
refund request
Client's request for money to be returned.record a refund request
refund approval
Authorized decision to return money under the relevant process.distinguish refund approval
checkout pause
Temporary stop in the payment process while a concern is addressed.confirm the checkout pause
service record
Record of what was discussed and performed.review the service record
acknowledgment
Clear recognition that the concern has been heard.give a specific acknowledgment
defensive response
Reply focused on resisting criticism rather than addressing the concern.avoid a defensive response
premature conclusion
Finding stated before the evidence or review supports it.avoid a premature conclusion
resolution status
Current stage of handling the concern.state the resolution status

Keep the next step distinct from the outcome. A mirror review is accepted, checkout is paused, and no correction or refund is approved. Further work requires a specific explanation and the relevant agreement, not an assumption that review implies consent.

C Notice the language

Acknowledge without deciding the finding

You can take a concern seriously without declaring either that the client is mistaken or that the stylist made an error. Repeat the observable concern, arrange a specific review, and keep the client informed about what has and has not been agreed.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge specifically
I hear that the left side looks longer to you.
Recognize the expectation
You were expecting an even outline.
Pause the transaction
Let us pause checkout while we address the concern.
Avoid dismissal
I will not assume it is just your imagination.
Avoid a finding too soon
We have not reviewed the appearance together yet.
Offer a concrete next step
Would you like a mirror review with Jo?
Clarify orientation
Do you mean your left side as you are sitting here?
Preserve the wording
I will pass on that it looks longer, not that a cause is confirmed.
Separate review and correction
Agreeing to review does not authorize more cutting.
Avoid a remedy promise
No correction has been approved yet.
Keep payment status accurate
No refund has been approved.
Invite the relevant explanation
Jo can review the concern against the agreed outline.
Check the record
We can refer to the consultation note during the review.
Confirm the accepted step
You would like the mirror review first.
Keep the client involved
Any proposed further work should be explained before agreement.
Close without premature resolution
The review is the next step; the concern is not yet resolved.
6 language and precision notes
Looks versus is
Looks longer reports perception; is longer asserts a confirmed comparison.
Your left
Clarifies orientation when a mirror or another person's viewpoint could confuse sides.
Review versus redo
Review examines the issue; redo implies additional service work.
Acknowledge versus admit
Acknowledging a concern does not automatically establish a fault finding.
Request versus approval
A requested refund and an authorized refund have different statuses.
First
First sequences review before any proposed remedy rather than guaranteeing a later outcome.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which opening is most useful?

Answer and explanation

B. I hear that the left side looks longer and that you expected an even outline.

The response acknowledges the specific concern and expectation without deciding cause or remedy.

2. Which statement confuses review with consent?

Answer and explanation

D. Agreeing to the mirror review means we can cut more without asking.

Consent to examine the result does not authorize additional cutting.

3. Which handover is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. Client reports a longer-looking left side; joint review pending.

The handover preserves the reported appearance and unfinished review status.

4. Which next step has been accepted?

Answer and explanation

C. Mirror review with Jo before deciding a remedy

The brief confirms review only and leaves both correction and refund unapproved.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

The left side looks longer

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mei: Client / Rafael: Receptionist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • reported concern
  • permission to cut
  • correction
  • cause
  • mirror review
  • even outline
  • pause checkout
  • pending
  • refund request
  • consultation note
  1. Mei
    Before I pay, could someone look at this side? It seems longer than the other one, and we agreed on an even outline.
  2. Rafael
    Of course. Let's 1 while we look into it. Is it your left side you're pointing to?
  3. Mei
    Yes, my left. It's easier to see when I look straight ahead. I don't know whether it's the angle.
  4. Rafael
    I'll ask Jo to join you for a 2. You can point out the section rather than relying on my description.
  5. Mei
    Please. I'm not asking for a different style; I want to check the outline we discussed.
  6. Rafael
    I'll make that clear. You expected an 3, and the left side currently looks longer to you.
  7. Mei
    Exactly. I was going to leave it, but I'd rather say something before I go home.
  8. Rafael
    Thank you for raising it. We can check the 4 with Jo and make sure we're discussing the same plan.
  9. Mei
    Do you think it needs more taken off? I don't want it getting shorter and shorter.
  10. Rafael
    We haven't established the 5 yet. Let's hear Jo's assessment before discussing whether further cutting is appropriate.
  11. Mei
    Yes, and please explain any proposed change first. I'd like to see exactly where it would be.
  12. Rafael
    Absolutely. Looking together isn't 6. You can ask questions before agreeing to any proposed work.
  13. Mei
    And if I'm still unhappy after we've looked at it, who can discuss the payment with me?
  14. Rafael
    We can take a 7 through our salon process. I haven't approved a refund by pausing checkout.
  15. Mei
    Okay. I wasn't sure what happened next. For now, I'd like Jo to see this side.
  16. Rafael
    I'll pass on the 8: your left looks longer, and you expected an even outline.
  17. Mei
    That's it. Please don't tell Jo I've asked for both sides to be cut shorter.
  18. Rafael
    I won't. A 9 hasn't been agreed. The first step is to review the appearance together.
  19. Mei
    Thank you. I'll show Jo what I mean in the mirror before we decide anything else.
  20. Rafael
    Yes. The review is still 10, and checkout stays paused while we arrange it.

Answers and explanations
  1. pause checkout Turn 2. Pausing checkout stops the payment process temporarily; it does not decide the complaint or refund.
  2. mirror review Turn 4. A mirror review lets Mei show Jo the specific area before a remedy is proposed.
  3. even outline Turn 6. The even outline was the reported consultation expectation, not a new style request.
  4. consultation note Turn 8. The consultation note helps compare the earlier plan with Mei's concern without pre-deciding the finding.
  5. cause Turn 10. The cause remains unknown until the relevant review; the receptionist should not invent it.
  6. permission to cut Turn 12. Permission to review the result does not authorize additional cutting without a separate agreement.
  7. refund request Turn 14. A refund request can be raised through the salon process but has not been approved here.
  8. reported concern Turn 16. The reported concern is that the client's left side looks longer, not that an error is already confirmed.
  9. correction Turn 18. A correction would be additional work; no particular correction has been proposed and accepted yet.
  10. pending Turn 20. Pending means the accepted review has not yet occurred; the concern is not recorded as resolved.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Mei: Before I pay, could someone look at this side? It seems longer than the other one, and we agreed on an even outline.
  2. Rafael: Of course. Let's pause checkout while we look into it. Is it your left side you're pointing to?
  3. Mei: Yes, my left. It's easier to see when I look straight ahead. I don't know whether it's the angle.
  4. Rafael: I'll ask Jo to join you for a mirror review. You can point out the section rather than relying on my description.
  5. Mei: Please. I'm not asking for a different style; I want to check the outline we discussed.
  6. Rafael: I'll make that clear. You expected an even outline, and the left side currently looks longer to you.
  7. Mei: Exactly. I was going to leave it, but I'd rather say something before I go home.
  8. Rafael: Thank you for raising it. We can check the consultation note with Jo and make sure we're discussing the same plan.
  9. Mei: Do you think it needs more taken off? I don't want it getting shorter and shorter.
  10. Rafael: We haven't established the cause yet. Let's hear Jo's assessment before discussing whether further cutting is appropriate.
  11. Mei: Yes, and please explain any proposed change first. I'd like to see exactly where it would be.
  12. Rafael: Absolutely. Looking together isn't permission to cut. You can ask questions before agreeing to any proposed work.
  13. Mei: And if I'm still unhappy after we've looked at it, who can discuss the payment with me?
  14. Rafael: We can take a refund request through our salon process. I haven't approved a refund by pausing checkout.
  15. Mei: Okay. I wasn't sure what happened next. For now, I'd like Jo to see this side.
  16. Rafael: I'll pass on the reported concern: your left looks longer, and you expected an even outline.
  17. Mei: That's it. Please don't tell Jo I've asked for both sides to be cut shorter.
  18. Rafael: I won't. A correction hasn't been agreed. The first step is to review the appearance together.
  19. Mei: Thank you. I'll show Jo what I mean in the mirror before we decide anything else.
  20. Rafael: Yes. The review is still pending, and checkout stays paused while we arrange it.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The left side looks longer

A client raises a concern before checkout.

  1. Stylist: You look uncertain about the result. Which area would you like to discuss together?
  2. Client: The left seems longer than the right. I expected the outline to look even.
  3. Stylist: Let's review the asymmetry you're noticing on the left before we continue with checkout.
  4. Client: Thank you. I wasn't sure whether mentioning it would sound as though I was complaining.
  5. Stylist: It's useful to be specific. Can you point to the section you mean?
  6. Client: This part near my jaw. In the front mirror it appears lower than the other side.
  7. Stylist: I see the area you're indicating. We should review the shape before discussing any adjustment.
  8. Client: Would that review automatically mean cutting more off both sides to make them match?
  9. Stylist: No. We haven't agreed on a change; we'll discuss any proposal with you first.
  10. Client: Please keep that distinction clear. I want to understand the options before losing more length.
A blunt message needs a correction

A receptionist repairs an earlier response to a service query.

  1. Receptionist: I said there was nothing we could do. That was too broad, and I'm sorry.
  2. Client: I took that to mean nobody would even listen to my concern about the finish.
  3. Receptionist: A service review is available. I should have distinguished that from a guaranteed refund.
  4. Client: I'm not asking you to decide the refund now. I want someone to hear me.
  5. Receptionist: The stylist can review your concern at four today. Would that time work?
  6. Client: Yes. Should I bring the photograph we discussed before the original appointment as well?
  7. Receptionist: Please do. It will help explain which part of the agreed look concerns you.
  8. Client: Will the review cost anything, or does booking it commit me to another service?
  9. Receptionist: The review is free. Any goodwill gesture needs the manager's agreement; no further service is booked.
  10. Client: Then four works. Thank you for correcting the impression rather than repeating the earlier refusal.
A concern after a different home finish

A client returns with a question about the haircut's appearance.

  1. Stylist: Tell me what looks different today compared with the finish you saw in the salon.
  2. Client: The front sits flatter after I washed it. I thought the volume was from the haircut.
  3. Stylist: So the difference concerns how the front sits, not its actual length today?
  4. Client: Yes. I don't think more length has disappeared; it simply doesn't look as full.
  5. Stylist: Thank you. The salon finish and the underlying cut are different things to discuss.
  6. Client: I wish that distinction had been clearer. I assumed it would look identical every morning.
  7. Stylist: I'm sorry we didn't explain that expectation clearly. Let's review the look you wanted.
  8. Client: Could we discuss options without automatically booking a new cut or buying a product?
  9. Stylist: Absolutely. This conversation is a review of your concern, not an agreement to another purchase.
  10. Client: That helps. I'd like to understand the difference before deciding whether to change anything.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The left side looks longer
  1. Read the result concern. Use your left to keep the client's orientation clear.
  2. Switch roles and read the review proposal, preserving the distinction between looking together and agreeing to more cutting.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Keep a second result concern factual

A client says the right side looks fuller than expected. An even outline was agreed. A mirror review with stylist Kai is accepted. Checkout is paused; neither further cutting nor a refund is approved.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Reception: "The reported concern is the ___ side."

Answer and explanation

A. right

The client identifies the right side in this new scenario.

2. Client: "We agreed on an ___ outline."

Answer and explanation

C. even

Even outline is the earlier expectation relevant to the review.

3. Reception: "The accepted next step is a mirror ___."

Answer and explanation

A. review

Review is accepted, while a particular remedy has not been authorized.

4. Reception: "No refund is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. approved

Pausing checkout and reviewing the concern do not establish refund approval.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 07: Responding to a concern about the result
Original fictional case: At checkout, client Mei says the left side of the haircut looks longer. Receptionist Rafael hears that the earlier consultation promised an even outline. The current appearance has not been reviewed together with stylist Jo. Rafael pauses checkout and offers a mirror review with Jo. Mei accepts the review. No cause has been established, and neither a correction nor a refund has been approved. The conversation must not turn a reported concern into a confirmed cutting error, a promise to remove more hair, or an automatic payment resolution.
Language workshop: The left side looks longer
Communication goal: Acknowledge a specific result concern, separate appearance from confirmed findings, and arrange review before promising a remedy.
Language guidance: Mei reports that the left side looks longer; no joint review has confirmed a cause or cutting error. The earlier expectation was an even outline. Acknowledging both facts does not authorize removing more hair.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08Passing preferences to the next appointmentRequested is not booked+

Your goal

Hand over confirmed preferences, an unbooked consultation request, and photography permission as separate facts.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Requested is not booked

Stylist Sora is handing client details to receptionist Jordan. The client requested a future color consultation but has not booked it or accepted a date. Confirmed preferences from today are retained front length and no finishing spray. No agreement to promotional photographs is recorded. Sora and Jordan must preserve those distinctions when updating the record: a preference is not a booking, a request is not an accepted appointment, and participation in a salon service does not establish permission to use an image for promotion.

Sora: Stylist / Jordan: Receptionist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What future service is requested?

Answer and explanation

B. A color consultation, not yet booked

The client requested a consultation but has not selected or accepted an appointment.

2. Which preferences are confirmed?

Answer and explanation

C. Retained front length and no finishing spray

The brief confirms only the two stated service preferences.

3. What is the photography status?

Answer and explanation

C. No promotional-photo agreement is recorded

No explicit agreement has been recorded, so permission must not be assumed.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The color consultation is requested but unbooked. Retained front length and no finishing spray are confirmed preferences. No promotional-photo agreement is recorded. These three statuses should not be compressed into a vague statement that the client agreed to everything.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
client profile
Record of relevant client information and preferences.update the client profile
preference note
Recorded choice about how the client wants a service handled.preserve a preference note
front-length retention
Keeping the specified hair length toward the face.confirm front-length retention
no-spray preference
Request not to use finishing spray.record the no-spray preference
consultation request
Expression of interest in a consultation, not a confirmed appointment.record the consultation request
pending booking
Appointment arrangement that is not yet confirmed.distinguish a pending booking
accepted date
Appointment date the client has explicitly agreed to.confirm the accepted date
follow-up contact
Later communication about an unresolved request.arrange follow-up contact
handover
Transfer of relevant information and responsibility between colleagues.give an accurate handover
read-back
Repetition of key details to confirm understanding.perform a read-back
record amendment
Correction or update to an existing entry.make a record amendment
service consent
Permission for a particular salon service.distinguish service consent
image permission
Agreement concerning taking or using a person's image.check image permission
promotional use
Use of material to advertise or market the salon.clarify promotional use
portfolio image
Image displayed to demonstrate work, subject to appropriate permission.discuss a portfolio image
publication channel
Place where content may be shared, such as a website.specify a publication channel
permission scope
Exact action or use covered by an agreement.confirm permission scope
recorded agreement
Agreement documented in the relevant record.check the recorded agreement
unconfirmed assumption
Belief not supported by what the client has agreed.remove an unconfirmed assumption
factual handover
Handover limited to accurate, relevant information.provide a factual handover
open request
Request still awaiting a decision or arrangement.track the open request
next contact owner
Person responsible for the next communication.identify the next contact owner
status label
Word identifying whether an item is requested, pending, or confirmed.use a precise status label
current record
Latest accurate version of the client information.verify the current record

Permission is purpose-specific. Do not infer promotional-image use from a completed service, a positive comment, or an unrecorded assumption. Use the actual salon process and applicable requirements for any later request involving taking or publishing images.

C Notice the language

Pass on statuses, not convenient assumptions

A quick handover can accidentally turn interest into a booking or silence into agreement. Label each item according to what the client actually said. Read back the pending request and confirmed preferences separately so the next colleague does not have to reconstruct the conversation.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Open the handover
I have two confirmed preferences and one unbooked request.
State the first preference
Please retain the front length.
State the second preference
The client does not want finishing spray.
Name the requested service
The client requested a future color consultation.
Keep the status clear
The consultation is requested but not booked.
Avoid inventing a date
No appointment date has been accepted.
Distinguish service and image permission
Service consent does not establish promotional-photo permission.
State the record accurately
No promotional-photo agreement is recorded.
Prevent an assumption
Please do not mark that request as confirmed.
Ask for a read-back
Can you repeat the confirmed preferences and pending request?
Correct a status
That should say requested, not booked.
Assign the next contact
Who will handle the next booking discussion?
Keep follow-up limited
Follow-up will address availability, not assume acceptance.
Keep the details separate
The no-spray preference is not a color-service decision.
Confirm the record
The profile should preserve these exact distinctions.
Close the handover
Two preferences confirmed; consultation unbooked; photo agreement absent.
6 language and precision notes
Requested versus booked
Interest or a request does not establish an accepted appointment.
Retain versus remove
Retain means keep the specified length rather than shorten it.
No spray
This is a product-use preference, not permission for every other product.
Recorded versus assumed
A documented agreement differs from a colleague's inference.
Purpose-specific permission
Agreement to a service does not automatically cover promotional use of an image.
Read-back
Repeating the status words catches errors that a general acknowledgment may miss.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which handover is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. Retain front length; no spray; color consultation requested but unbooked.

The correct handover preserves both confirmed preferences and the unbooked request.

2. What does no promotional-photo agreement recorded mean?

Answer and explanation

C. Do not treat promotional use as authorized.

Absence of a recorded agreement does not supply permission for promotional use.

3. Which label should replace an inaccurate booked entry?

Answer and explanation

C. Requested, not booked

Requested reflects the client's interest without inventing an accepted date.

4. Which read-back catches a meaningful error?

Answer and explanation

C. No date accepted; the consultation remains unbooked.

The specific read-back verifies the unresolved booking status rather than offering a vague acknowledgment.

Check explanations in the book, page 99 ↗

E Conversations

Requested is not booked

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Sora: Stylist / Jordan: Receptionist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • Service consent
  • unbooked
  • retain
  • recorded
  • no-spray preference
  • follow-up contact
  • read-back
  • booking status
  • client profile
  • consultation request
  1. Sora
    Could we check the client notes before you take over? There are two preferences and a color enquiry to pass on.
  2. Jordan
    Yes. I'll open the 1. What should I put first?
  3. Sora
    Keep the front length, and no finishing spray. Please don't shorten that to just natural-looking.
  4. Jordan
    I'll write 2 front length. Does no spray refer specifically to finishing spray?
  5. Sora
    It does. Those are the client's words. We didn't agree to a different product instead.
  6. Jordan
    Then the 3 gets its own line. That should be easier for the next stylist to spot.
  7. Sora
    The client also asked about a future color consultation. We haven't picked a date or booked anything.
  8. Jordan
    So a 4, not a color treatment booking. Did you offer a particular time?
  9. Sora
    No. Reception needs to discuss availability with the client. I don't want a reminder going out before that happens.
  10. Jordan
    I'll leave it 5. I'll handle the availability discussion, then confirm any choice with the client.
  11. Sora
    One more thing: there's no agreement to promotional photos in the record. Please don't mark that as permission.
  12. Jordan
    Understood. 6 doesn't cover a promotional image. We need the appropriate separate conversation for that.
  13. Sora
    Right. If someone asks about a portfolio picture later, the note mustn't suggest we've already dealt with it.
  14. Jordan
    I'll state that no photo agreement is 7, rather than guessing which uses the client would accept.
  15. Sora
    Can you read the summary back? The booked-versus-requested bit is the one I'm most concerned about.
  16. Jordan
    Here's the 8: retain front length, no finishing spray, future color consultation requested but unbooked; no promotional-photo agreement recorded.
  17. Sora
    Yes, that's right. The client hasn't agreed to a date, a color formula, or an alternative finishing product.
  18. Jordan
    I'll keep the 9 separate from the preference notes. The consultation remains a follow-up request.
  19. Sora
    Thank you. Please make sure whoever sees the next appointment has those preferences in front of them.
  20. Jordan
    I'll update the record and take the 10. Any offered slot still needs the client's acceptance.

Answers and explanations
  1. client profile Turn 2. The client profile holds the relevant preferences and requests, rather than a new confirmed booking.
  2. retain Turn 4. Retain means keep the front length, the opposite of cutting it shorter.
  3. no-spray preference Turn 6. This preference records the explicit product exclusion, without authorizing a substitute.
  4. consultation request Turn 8. The request concerns a future discussion, not an accepted color service or appointment.
  5. unbooked Turn 10. Unbooked is accurate because no appointment date has been offered and accepted.
  6. Service consent Turn 12. Permission for the salon service does not itself authorize promotional photographs or their publication.
  7. recorded Turn 14. Recorded refers to documented agreement; its absence cannot be replaced by an assumption.
  8. read-back Turn 16. A read-back repeats the actual choices and statuses so a colleague can catch changed meaning.
  9. booking status Turn 18. Booking status remains separate from confirmed preferences, so an enquiry cannot become a booking by accident.
  10. follow-up contact Turn 20. Follow-up contact is the next availability conversation, not an assertion that an appointment already exists.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Sora: Could we check the client notes before you take over? There are two preferences and a color enquiry to pass on.
  2. Jordan: Yes. I'll open the client profile. What should I put first?
  3. Sora: Keep the front length, and no finishing spray. Please don't shorten that to just natural-looking.
  4. Jordan: I'll write retain front length. Does no spray refer specifically to finishing spray?
  5. Sora: It does. Those are the client's words. We didn't agree to a different product instead.
  6. Jordan: Then the no-spray preference gets its own line. That should be easier for the next stylist to spot.
  7. Sora: The client also asked about a future color consultation. We haven't picked a date or booked anything.
  8. Jordan: So a consultation request, not a color treatment booking. Did you offer a particular time?
  9. Sora: No. Reception needs to discuss availability with the client. I don't want a reminder going out before that happens.
  10. Jordan: I'll leave it unbooked. I'll handle the availability discussion, then confirm any choice with the client.
  11. Sora: One more thing: there's no agreement to promotional photos in the record. Please don't mark that as permission.
  12. Jordan: Understood. Service consent doesn't cover a promotional image. We need the appropriate separate conversation for that.
  13. Sora: Right. If someone asks about a portfolio picture later, the note mustn't suggest we've already dealt with it.
  14. Jordan: I'll state that no photo agreement is recorded, rather than guessing which uses the client would accept.
  15. Sora: Can you read the summary back? The booked-versus-requested bit is the one I'm most concerned about.
  16. Jordan: Here's the read-back: retain front length, no finishing spray, future color consultation requested but unbooked; no promotional-photo agreement recorded.
  17. Sora: Yes, that's right. The client hasn't agreed to a date, a color formula, or an alternative finishing product.
  18. Jordan: I'll keep the booking status separate from the preference notes. The consultation remains a follow-up request.
  19. Sora: Thank you. Please make sure whoever sees the next appointment has those preferences in front of them.
  20. Jordan: I'll update the record and take the follow-up contact. Any offered slot still needs the client's acceptance.
Dialogue in the book, page 73 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A color inquiry reaches reception

A stylist hands a client visit over to reception.

  1. Stylist: Today's client kept the front length and chose no finishing spray. Please preserve both preferences.
  2. Receptionist: I've noted those. Is the color consultation already booked, or was it only discussed today?
  3. Stylist: Only discussed. The client asked about future dates, but hasn't selected one yet.
  4. Receptionist: Then I should offer consultation times, not describe the next visit as confirmed color treatment.
  5. Stylist: Exactly. Please check the client's choice directly rather than carrying a date over from another visit.
  6. Receptionist: Is there any permission for a promotional photograph with the new haircut this afternoon?
  7. Stylist: No permission has been given. Don't interpret the service note as agreement to photography.
  8. Receptionist: Understood. I'll ask only about booking times now and keep any photo request separate.
  9. Stylist: Please tell me which consultation date is accepted, or leave it clearly unbooked.
  10. Receptionist: I will. The next task is offering dates; neither color service nor photography is agreed.
Keeping an internal photograph private

A stylist clarifies a client's limited photo permission.

  1. Stylist: Would you like a reference photo in your client record for the next consultation?
  2. Client: Yes, that would help me remember the outline, but I don't want it posted publicly.
  3. Stylist: So you're agreeing to the stated record use only, not social media or promotional material.
  4. Client: That's right. I saw the salon's photo display and wanted to make the difference clear.
  5. Stylist: Thank you. We'll handle the image under our record arrangements and note that limited permission.
  6. Client: Can I choose not to have any photo taken after all if I feel uncomfortable?
  7. Stylist: Yes. We can use a descriptive service note instead; a photograph isn't required for rebooking.
  8. Client: Actually, I'd prefer the note today. Please describe the retained length and the front shape.
  9. Stylist: No photograph today, then. I'll preserve those preferences in the note for the next stylist.
  10. Client: That suits me. I'm glad agreeing to an appointment doesn't require agreeing to a picture.
A new barber checks an old note

A covering barber discusses a returning client's recorded preference.

  1. Barber: Your previous note says short sides. Is that still what you want for today's visit?
  2. Client: Not this time. I'm growing the sides out and only want to discuss the neckline.
  3. Barber: Thank you. The old service note describes that visit, not a permanent instruction for every haircut.
  4. Client: Could you keep the older note but make today's changed preference clear for next time?
  5. Barber: Yes. Today's discussion will state that you're growing the sides and reviewing the neckline only.
  6. Client: There's also an old note about beard trimming. I don't want that service today either.
  7. Barber: I'll keep that separate. No beard trim is requested as part of this appointment.
  8. Client: Can we check the visible outline before anything starts, even though I'm a returning client?
  9. Barber: Certainly. Returning doesn't remove the need to confirm what you want on this particular visit.
  10. Client: Then let's look at the neckline together and keep both the sides and beard unchanged.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Requested is not booked
  1. Read the handover. Emphasize retain front length and no finishing spray as separate preferences.
  2. Switch roles and repeat the read-back exactly, preserving requested but unbooked and no photo agreement recorded.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Read back a new client handover

The client confirms retained side length and no finishing spray. A future styling consultation is requested but unbooked. Reception will handle the next availability discussion. No promotional-image agreement is recorded.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Stylist: "Please retain the ___ length."

Answer and explanation

A. side

The confirmed retained-length preference concerns the sides in this new case.

2. Reception: "The consultation is requested but ___."

Answer and explanation

B. unbooked

No accepted appointment has been established by the request alone.

3. Stylist: "The next availability discussion belongs to ___."

Answer and explanation

A. reception

Reception is explicitly assigned the next contact in the supplied handover.

4. Reception: "No promotional-image agreement is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. recorded

The record contains no agreement authorizing promotional image use.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 08: Passing preferences to the next appointment
Original fictional case: Stylist Sora is handing client details to receptionist Jordan. The client requested a future color consultation but has not booked it or accepted a date. Confirmed preferences from today are retained front length and no finishing spray. No agreement to promotional photographs is recorded. Sora and Jordan must preserve those distinctions when updating the record: a preference is not a booking, a request is not an accepted appointment, and participation in a salon service does not establish permission to use an image for promotion.
Language workshop: Requested is not booked
Communication goal: Hand over confirmed preferences, an unbooked consultation request, and photography permission as separate facts.
Language guidance: The color consultation is requested but unbooked. Retained front length and no finishing spray are confirmed preferences. No promotional-photo agreement is recorded. These three statuses should not be compressed into a vague statement that the client agreed to everything.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Language workshop: Requested is not booked
Communication goal: Hand over confirmed preferences, an unbooked consultation request, and photography permission as separate facts.
Language guidance: The color consultation is requested but unbooked. Retained front length and no finishing spray are confirmed preferences. No promotional-photo agreement is recorded. These three statuses should not be compressed into a vague statement that the client agreed to everything.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I have two confirmed preferences and one unbooked request. / Please retain the front length. / The client does not want finishing spray. / The client requested a future color consultation. / The consultation is requested but not booked. / No appointment date has been accepted. / Service consent does not establish promotional-photo permission. / No promotional-photo agreement is recorded. / Please do not mark that request as confirmed. / Can you repeat the confirmed preferences and pending request? / That should say requested, not booked. / Who will handle the next booking discussion? / Follow-up will address availability, not assume acceptance. / The no-spray preference is not a color-service decision. / The profile should preserve these exact distinctions. / Two preferences confirmed; consultation unbooked; photo agreement absent.
Vocabulary: client profile: Record of relevant client information and preferences. / preference note: Recorded choice about how the client wants a service handled. / front-length retention: Keeping the specified hair length toward the face. / no-spray preference: Request not to use finishing spray. / consultation request: Expression of interest in a consultation, not a confirmed appointment. / pending booking: Appointment arrangement that is not yet confirmed. / accepted date: Appointment date the client has explicitly agreed to. / follow-up contact: Later communication about an unresolved request. / handover: Transfer of relevant information and responsibility between colleagues. / read-back: Repetition of key details to confirm understanding. / record amendment: Correction or update to an existing entry. / service consent: Permission for a particular salon service. / image permission: Agreement concerning taking or using a person's image. / promotional use: Use of material to advertise or market the salon. / portfolio image: Image displayed to demonstrate work, subject to appropriate permission. / publication channel: Place where content may be shared, such as a website. / permission scope: Exact action or use covered by an agreement. / recorded agreement: Agreement documented in the relevant record. / unconfirmed assumption: Belief not supported by what the client has agreed. / factual handover: Handover limited to accurate, relevant information. / open request: Request still awaiting a decision or arrangement. / next contact owner: Person responsible for the next communication. / status label: Word identifying whether an item is requested, pending, or confirmed. / current record: Latest accurate version of the client information.
Speaking task: Read the handover. Emphasize retain front length and no finishing spray as separate preferences. Switch roles and repeat the read-back exactly, preserving requested but unbooked and no photo agreement recorded. Complete and check the transfer. Keep retained side length distinct from the front-length preference in the longer exchange.
Partner: Sora: Stylist / Jordan: Receptionist
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: The client confirms retained side length and no finishing spray. A future styling consultation is requested but unbooked. Reception will handle the next availability discussion. No promotional-image agreement is recorded.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Sora: Yes, that's right. The client hasn't agreed to a date, a color formula, or an alternative finishing product.
Jordan: I'll keep the booking status separate from the preference notes. The consultation remains a follow-up request.
Sora: Thank you. Please make sure whoever sees the next appointment has those preferences in front of them.
Jordan: I'll update the record and take the follow-up contact. Any offered slot still needs the client's acceptance.
END REFERENCE

More situations, different skills

Three additional workplace scenarios

Length in the way you wear it

Distinguish visible curl length, strand thickness, and density when agreeing on a shape.

Client Nia wears her hair in its natural curls. Stretched, it reaches below her shoulders; dry and unstretched, it sits at shoulder level. She wants to keep that dry shoulder-length outline and more crown volume. The consultation records fine individual strands and high density. No amount to remove or cutting method is agreed.

Nia: Client / Amir: Stylist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • crown volume
  • shrinkage
  • dry outline
  • Strand thickness
  • density
  • natural fall
  1. Nia
    When I say shoulder-length, I mean how it sits when I leave it curly. Last time, the wet length looked right but the dry result felt too short.
  2. Amir
    Then let's use your 1 as the reference. You want the bottom to stay at shoulder level when worn naturally.
  3. Nia
    Exactly. If I pull a section straight, it goes below my shoulders. That's not how I wear it during the day.
  4. Amir
    That difference is 2. We need to account for how your curls sit, without assuming every section springs back equally.
  5. Nia
    I'd like more height at the top, though. Does that mean losing the length around the bottom?
  6. Amir
    Your requested 3 is a separate goal. Let's discuss possible shape changes before deciding how much, if anything, comes off each area.
  7. Nia
    People call my hair thick, but the individual hairs feel quite fine. Is that a contradiction?
  8. Amir
    No. 4 describes each hair. Density describes how much hair grows in an area. Your consultation records fine strands with high density.
  9. Nia
    That explains it. I have a lot of hair, but I don't want a heavy product recommendation based just on the word thick.
  10. Amir
    Agreed. We'll keep 5 separate from strand thickness and your styling preferences. Neither description alone chooses a product for you.
  11. Nia
    The curls at the front sit differently from the back. Please don't assume the whole head behaves like one section.
  12. Amir
    I won't. We can compare the areas in their 6 and discuss the shape you actually wear.
  13. Nia
    Would it help if I showed you where I want the bottom edge in the mirror?
  14. Amir
    Yes. That gives us a shared visual reference. It still doesn't turn shoulder-length into a fixed number of centimeters to remove everywhere.
  15. Nia
    Right. I'm asking to keep that edge, not to cut every wet section to the same shoulder line.
  16. Amir
    Understood. We haven't agreed on a cutting method or an amount yet. First we'll confirm the shape and the length you want protected.
  17. Nia
    And the crown goal is more volume, not straightening. I want to keep wearing the curls.
  18. Amir
    That's clear. I'll record natural curls, retained dry shoulder-length outline, and a discussion of crown volume. No straightening service has been requested.
  19. Nia
    Please read that back before we start, including the dry-length point. That's the part I don't want lost.
  20. Amir
    Certainly. Shoulder-level when dry and unstretched; more crown volume to discuss; fine strands, high density; cutting details still to be agreed.

Answers and explanations
  1. dry outline Turn 2. The dry outline is the visible shoulder-level shape Nia wants to retain, not the longer stretched endpoint.
  2. shrinkage Turn 4. Shrinkage describes the shorter visible length when curls recoil, not missing hair or a measured amount to cut.
  3. crown volume Turn 6. Crown volume concerns fullness at the upper back of the head, separate from the retained outer length.
  4. Strand thickness Turn 8. Strand thickness describes individual hairs; fine strands can occur in a head with many hairs.
  5. density Turn 10. Density concerns the amount of hair in an area, not the diameter of each strand or a product instruction.
  6. natural fall Turn 12. Natural fall is how the hair sits without being held stretched, which is the relevant appearance for this request.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Nia: When I say shoulder-length, I mean how it sits when I leave it curly. Last time, the wet length looked right but the dry result felt too short.
  2. Amir: Then let's use your dry outline as the reference. You want the bottom to stay at shoulder level when worn naturally.
  3. Nia: Exactly. If I pull a section straight, it goes below my shoulders. That's not how I wear it during the day.
  4. Amir: That difference is shrinkage. We need to account for how your curls sit, without assuming every section springs back equally.
  5. Nia: I'd like more height at the top, though. Does that mean losing the length around the bottom?
  6. Amir: Your requested crown volume is a separate goal. Let's discuss possible shape changes before deciding how much, if anything, comes off each area.
  7. Nia: People call my hair thick, but the individual hairs feel quite fine. Is that a contradiction?
  8. Amir: No. Strand thickness describes each hair. Density describes how much hair grows in an area. Your consultation records fine strands with high density.
  9. Nia: That explains it. I have a lot of hair, but I don't want a heavy product recommendation based just on the word thick.
  10. Amir: Agreed. We'll keep density separate from strand thickness and your styling preferences. Neither description alone chooses a product for you.
  11. Nia: The curls at the front sit differently from the back. Please don't assume the whole head behaves like one section.
  12. Amir: I won't. We can compare the areas in their natural fall and discuss the shape you actually wear.
  13. Nia: Would it help if I showed you where I want the bottom edge in the mirror?
  14. Amir: Yes. That gives us a shared visual reference. It still doesn't turn shoulder-length into a fixed number of centimeters to remove everywhere.
  15. Nia: Right. I'm asking to keep that edge, not to cut every wet section to the same shoulder line.
  16. Amir: Understood. We haven't agreed on a cutting method or an amount yet. First we'll confirm the shape and the length you want protected.
  17. Nia: And the crown goal is more volume, not straightening. I want to keep wearing the curls.
  18. Amir: That's clear. I'll record natural curls, retained dry shoulder-length outline, and a discussion of crown volume. No straightening service has been requested.
  19. Nia: Please read that back before we start, including the dry-length point. That's the part I don't want lost.
  20. Amir: Certainly. Shoulder-level when dry and unstretched; more crown volume to discuss; fine strands, high density; cutting details still to be agreed.
Protect the unstretched endpoint

A client wants chin-level hair when dry and unstretched, not the collarbone endpoint seen when stretched. Individual hairs are coarse, but density is low. No cutting amount is agreed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Client: Keep the dry outline at my ___ .

Answer and explanation

D. chin

The selected endpoint is the chin in the dry unstretched state, not the stretched collarbone position.

2. Stylist: The longer comparison shows the hair ___ .

Answer and explanation

B. stretched

The collarbone endpoint belongs to the stretched comparison, not the requested natural outline.

3. Client: The individual strands are ___ .

Answer and explanation

B. coarse

Coarse describes individual strand thickness, not how many hairs grow in an area.

4. Stylist: The recorded density is ___ .

Answer and explanation

A. low

Low density concerns the amount of hair in an area and can coexist with coarse strands.

Check the answers, then read both exchanges aloud. Swap roles on the second reading.

A number needs a length

Read a tool-specific guard chart and distinguish length left from length removed.

For this consultation, the shop's identified hair-clipper chart lists nominal lengths: #1 = 3 mm, #2 = 6 mm, #3 = 10 mm, #4 = 13 mm. Client Pavel first says number two, thinking it means removing 2 mm. He clarifies a preference around 13 mm at the sides. Top, neckline, and beard remain unagreed; no cutting begins.

Pavel: Client / Leah: Barber

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • guide comb
  • number two
  • millimeters
  • separate agreement
  • number four
  • left
  1. Pavel
    Could you do a number two on the sides? I only want a little off, not a really close cut.
  2. Leah
    Let's check what you mean. On this hair-clipper chart, 1 is listed as six millimeters remaining. Were you expecting that length?
  3. Pavel
    Oh, no. I thought two meant taking off two millimeters. I'd like the sides much longer than six.
  4. Leah
    The chart describes the length 2, not the amount removed. It's worth checking before we choose a guard.
  5. Pavel
    About thirteen millimeters sounds nearer what I meant. Which number is that on your chart?
  6. Leah
    That's 3. We'll discuss the appearance at that length before treating the number as the whole haircut plan.
  7. Pavel
    And number three is ten millimeters? I can see the printed lengths now. They're not counting up one millimeter at a time.
  8. Leah
    Correct. Read the stated 4, not just the sequence of numbers. These are the listed nominal lengths for this identified tool system.
  9. Pavel
    My beard trimmer has numbers too. Could I use the same number and expect exactly the same result?
  10. Leah
    Not automatically. Check that tool's 5 and instructions. A remembered number from another device isn't enough to identify a matching length.
  11. Pavel
    So if I come back, I should mention the length as well as the number. That would avoid this mix-up.
  12. Leah
    Yes, along with the areas and appearance you liked. Tool settings and the final look still need checking on the actual hair.
  13. Pavel
    Does asking for thirteen millimeters at the sides mean you'll cut the top to thirteen as well?
  14. Leah
    No. We need a 6 about the top. The neckline and your beard haven't been discussed either.
  15. Pavel
    Please leave the beard out of today's haircut conversation for now. I haven't decided what I want done with it.
  16. Leah
    Understood. No beard work is agreed. We'll finish the haircut consultation without adding that service by assumption.
  17. Pavel
    And is thirteen a promise that every single hair will measure exactly thirteen afterward?
  18. Leah
    No. It's the chart's nominal length, not a guarantee about every hair. We still need to agree the shape and appropriate professional approach.
  19. Pavel
    Then my original number-two request was wrong. Please record the longer side preference and check the remaining details with me.
  20. Leah
    I will: around thirteen millimeters at the sides, number four on this chart as our reference, with the rest of the plan still to confirm.

Answers and explanations
  1. number two Turn 2. Number two corresponds to a nominal 6 mm length on this specified chart, not removal of 2 mm.
  2. left Turn 4. Left means retained after cutting, whereas Pavel initially understood the number as an amount removed.
  3. number four Turn 6. The supplied chart assigns 13 mm to number four; the number is not a centimeter measurement.
  4. millimeters Turn 8. The millimeter labels provide the actual nominal lengths; guard numbers are identifiers rather than a one-millimeter scale.
  5. guide comb Turn 10. The guide comb and its own documentation identify the length; another trimmer need not use the same numbering.
  6. separate agreement Turn 14. A side-length preference does not settle the top, neckline, or beard, which are expressly unagreed.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Pavel: Could you do a number two on the sides? I only want a little off, not a really close cut.
  2. Leah: Let's check what you mean. On this hair-clipper chart, number two is listed as six millimeters remaining. Were you expecting that length?
  3. Pavel: Oh, no. I thought two meant taking off two millimeters. I'd like the sides much longer than six.
  4. Leah: The chart describes the length left, not the amount removed. It's worth checking before we choose a guard.
  5. Pavel: About thirteen millimeters sounds nearer what I meant. Which number is that on your chart?
  6. Leah: That's number four. We'll discuss the appearance at that length before treating the number as the whole haircut plan.
  7. Pavel: And number three is ten millimeters? I can see the printed lengths now. They're not counting up one millimeter at a time.
  8. Leah: Correct. Read the stated millimeters, not just the sequence of numbers. These are the listed nominal lengths for this identified tool system.
  9. Pavel: My beard trimmer has numbers too. Could I use the same number and expect exactly the same result?
  10. Leah: Not automatically. Check that tool's guide comb and instructions. A remembered number from another device isn't enough to identify a matching length.
  11. Pavel: So if I come back, I should mention the length as well as the number. That would avoid this mix-up.
  12. Leah: Yes, along with the areas and appearance you liked. Tool settings and the final look still need checking on the actual hair.
  13. Pavel: Does asking for thirteen millimeters at the sides mean you'll cut the top to thirteen as well?
  14. Leah: No. We need a separate agreement about the top. The neckline and your beard haven't been discussed either.
  15. Pavel: Please leave the beard out of today's haircut conversation for now. I haven't decided what I want done with it.
  16. Leah: Understood. No beard work is agreed. We'll finish the haircut consultation without adding that service by assumption.
  17. Pavel: And is thirteen a promise that every single hair will measure exactly thirteen afterward?
  18. Leah: No. It's the chart's nominal length, not a guarantee about every hair. We still need to agree the shape and appropriate professional approach.
  19. Pavel: Then my original number-two request was wrong. Please record the longer side preference and check the remaining details with me.
  20. Leah: I will: around thirteen millimeters at the sides, number four on this chart as our reference, with the rest of the plan still to confirm.
Read another chart entry

Use the same chart: #1 = 3 mm, #2 = 6 mm, #3 = 10 mm, #4 = 13 mm. A client wants approximately 10 mm remaining at the sides. The beard has not been discussed. Interpret the request only; no technique is supplied.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Client: I want approximately ___ millimeters remaining at the sides.

Answer and explanation

A. ten

Ten millimeters is the requested retained length, not the amount removed.

2. Barber: On this chart, that is guard number ___ .

Answer and explanation

A. three

Number three corresponds to 10 mm on the supplied tool-specific chart.

3. Client: The chart describes length left, not length ___ .

Answer and explanation

B. removed

Remaining length and the amount taken off are different measurements.

4. Barber: Work on the beard is still ___ .

Answer and explanation

A. unagreed

A side-length request does not authorize a separate, undiscussed beard service.

Check the answers, then read both exchanges aloud. Swap roles on the second reading.
